# Gantry Engineering Spec

The single source of truth for contracts. Every issue references sections here by number. If an issue conflicts with this spec, follow the spec and flag it in the PR. To change a contract, edit this file in the same PR and say so in the PR description.

Plan doc (background and rationale): https://claude.ai/artifact/7UCZZRCs4k45gPkKBfocmY

## 1. Ground rules

- **Repo:** `jbknick/architecture-visualization-cc-plugin` (public). The product's display name is **Claude Code Architecture Visualisation Plugin**; the CLI command, package scope, MCP server name, plugin id and data directory keep the short identifier **`gantry`**. Default branch `main`. One branch and PR per issue (`gantry/<issue-number>-<short-slug>`), stacked on its dependency's branch when that one isn't merged yet.
- **Public repo:** the test bed is a private evaluation project. Never commit its code, fact sheets, descriptions, graph dumps or screenshots showing its code. Private eval data lives only in git-ignored `evals/private/`. Commit only scripts and aggregate numbers.
- **Toolchain:** Node 26 (`.nvmrc`, `engines.node` `>=26 <27`), pnpm 10.31.0 (`packageManager`, `engines.pnpm`; `pnpm check` fails when the running Node major differs from `.nvmrc` or the pins disagree), TypeScript strict, ESM (`"module": "NodeNext"`), vitest, Biome. Build with `tsc -b`; bundle CLI/MCP entry points with `tsup` into `plugin/dist/`.
- **Local quality gate:** `pnpm check` = `biome check . && tsc -b && vitest run` (plus the version, evals and describe-mutation checks in `package.json`). `tsc -b` type-checks and writes the git-ignored `dist/` output; TypeScript 7 rejects `--noEmit` on referenced composite projects (TS6310). Biome follows git's ignore rules (`.gitignore` and `info/exclude`), so untracked local files do not fail it. The description mutation check runs on a temporary copy and never edits tracked files, so several checkouts and worktrees can run the gate at once. It must pass before a PR. This repo has no CI: never add a workflow under `.github/workflows/` or wait for GitHub Actions. The composite Action in `action/` (#282, sections 3, 10 and 14) is something Gantry ships for users' repositories, not a gate for this one; Gantry never writes a workflow file into a target repo, the user adds it. Live runs of the Action happen in a separate sandbox repository, never here.
- **Uploads are opt-in and never carry source.** Nothing is sent to a team server unless the user links a checkout (section 5, "Team config") and runs an upload. An upload carries names, paths, line ranges, edges and hashes, never a function body, a signature, comments, file contents or a fact sheet (section 20, "Rules"). `core/src/team/upload.ts` builds the two payloads (section 11, "Upload payloads") and `upload.guard.test.ts` fails when any field could carry source. A payload equals what a dry run prints, because one function builds both.
- **Determinism:** index output is sorted and stable; no timestamps or random values inside graph content (timestamps only in `meta`, `usage_log`, `created_at`).
- **Target repo is read-only** except `.git/hooks/*` and local git config written by `gantry hooks install`. Recipes (`.gantry/recipe.json`) are written by hand and committed by the user; Gantry only reads them (section 5, recipe) and checks them with `gantry recipe check --nodes`. Never write any other file into a target repo's working tree.
- **No framework- or ORM-specific code (#203):** what a stack's routes, endpoints and tables look like lives in the repo's recipe (`.gantry/recipe.json`, section 5), applied the same way on every index run. New stack knowledge is a recipe rule or a generic helper, never a new adapter. Data access follows the same rule (#247): tables, and the functions that read and write them, come from recipe rules and four generic helpers (`sql-text`, `text-rules`, `model-naming`, `method-classes`) for any ORM or raw SQL, and no ORM or driver name appears in the engine or a helper. The built-in adapters that predate recipes (TanStack Router, TanStack Start, Hono, oRPC, Drizzle) are kept and stay registered (see section 9); recipes add to them, and nothing plans to remove them. `recipe/data-access.test.ts` proves, for Drizzle, that a recipe alone writes what the built-in adapter writes.
- **Process I/O:** hooks and the MCP server log to stderr only. stdout is reserved for protocol / JSON output.
- **Fail open:** git hooks and Claude Code hooks must never block a commit or a tool call because Gantry failed. Catch, log to `<dataDir>/logs/`, exit 0.
- **Worker guard:** if env `GANTRY_WORKER=1`, every hook entry point exits 0 immediately with no output.
- **Dependencies:** only those in section 2 without justification in the PR.
- **Releases (#281):** a release carries two tarballs, each with its own `.sha256` file: `gantry-<version>.tgz` for every platform, and `gantry-protocol-<version>.tgz`, the `@gantry/protocol` package, for any consumer that pins the protocol by URL and checksum. Gantry is never published to a package registry. The CLI tarball holds the bundled CLI and a lockfile for the native dependencies the bundle leaves external, which a runner installs with `npm ci`. The maintainer tags, pushes and uploads; no script does.

## 2. Approved dependencies

Runtime: `oxc-parser`, `oxc-resolver`, `better-sqlite3`, `sqlite-vec` (pin exact version), `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`, `zod`, `commander`, `ignore`, `picomatch`, `@huggingface/transformers`, `@ast-grep/napi`, `@ast-grep/lang-go`, `@ast-grep/lang-java`, `@ast-grep/lang-python`, `@ast-grep/lang-csharp` and `@ast-grep/lang-rust` (#221; exact pins `0.51.3`, `0.0.6`, `0.0.7`, `0.0.6`, `0.0.6` and `0.0.7`, each in `packages/core`, the root `package.json` and `scripts/release-deps/` (its `package.json` and `package-lock.json`), and `external` in `tsup.config.ts`), `sigma`, `graphology`, `graphology-layout-forceatlas2`, `elkjs`, `react`, `react-dom`, `3d-force-graph`, `three`, `three-spritetext`. Later only: `ts-morph`. Dropped in M4 (#40): `@parcel/watcher` and `ws`. The explorer is not live; it refreshes on request (section 11).
Explorer UI kit (`packages/explorer/web`, #252): `tailwindcss` and `@tailwindcss/vite` (Tailwind CSS 4, utilities and theme only, no preflight), `motion` (the animations of the SmoothUI components), `clsx` and `tailwind-merge` (the `cn` helper), `@base-ui/react` (the Base UI primitives under the shadcn `base-nova` style), and `@hugeicons/react` with `@hugeicons/core-free-icons` (MIT; the only icon source; `@hugeicons-pro` is not allowed, its licence does not permit open-source icon assets). SmoothUI and shadcn components are copied as source into `web/src/components/smoothui/` and `web/src/components/ui/` and listed in `web/src/components/smoothui/SOURCES.md`; the shadcn CLI runs through `pnpm dlx` and is not a dependency. `class-variance-authority` is not approved: copied primitives use plain lookup tables instead.
Explorer 3D map (`packages/explorer/web`, lazy chunk only): `3d-force-graph` gives the orbit camera, picking and link rendering for the `map-3d` view; `three` is its renderer and draws the workspace islands and bundled arcs; `three-spritetext` makes the always-facing text labels. The default `settlements` style of the same view (#114) is a plain `three` scene built from the project map's files-level data: one settlement per workspace (village, town, city or metropolis by function count), one procedural instanced building per file, roads and highways for workspace imports with instanced traffic, `OrbitControls` from `three/examples`, DOM labels; no new dependency, no downloaded models or textures. `style=city` selects the code city (instanced boxes and tube arches on the 2D project map's layout) and `style=islands` the island rendering above.
The recipe engine (#203, `core/src/recipe/`) runs recipe rules with `@ast-grep/napi` (exact pin `0.51.3`, already approved for Go) on TypeScript and TSX, and (#368) on Go through the `@ast-grep/lang-go` grammar that `lang/packs/go.ts` names; no other dependency was added. The package ships prebuilt binaries for macOS arm64 and x64, Linux arm64 and x64 and Windows x64; there is none for Windows arm64.
Pack-language parsing (Go since #221, Java, Python, C# and Rust after it) loads each pack's tree-sitter grammar (the pack's `grammar.package`: `@ast-grep/lang-go`, `@ast-grep/lang-java`, `@ast-grep/lang-python`, `@ast-grep/lang-csharp`, `@ast-grep/lang-rust`) at runtime through `registerDynamicLanguage`, once per process with every registered pack in that one call; the node kinds that are read come from our own tables in the pack (`nodes`, `tokens`), not from tree-sitter query files, and no tree-sitter runtime is a dependency; pnpm 10 skips that package's `postinstall` and it is not allowed, because the tarball ships the parsers. Prebuilt platforms: macOS arm64 and x64, Linux arm64 and x64, Windows x64. Windows arm64 has no Go, Java, Python, C# or Rust prebuild: there `gantry` prints `go: no tree-sitter Go prebuild for <platform>; Go files skipped` (and the same line for Java, for Python, for C# and for Rust) once, indexes no `.go`, `.java`, `.py`, `.pyi`, `.cs` or `.rs` file and no module, project, package or crate of that language, and the other languages go on. The Go toolchain, the JDK, a Python interpreter, the .NET SDK and Cargo are never needed or run by indexing (no `child_process` in `lang/engine/`); the Go toolchain is used only to produce the ground truth of `evals/go/` (`docs/spikes/v2-go/truth`).
Bundled asset (not a dependency): the English word list of section 8, generated from `/usr/share/dict/words` (Webster's Second International, 1934, copyright lapsed, public domain) by `scripts/build-wordlist.ts` into `packages/core/src/describe/wordlist.data.ts` (582 KB).
Dev: `typescript`, `vitest`, `@biomejs/biome`, `tsup`, `tsx`, `vite`, `@vitejs/plugin-react`, `playwright` (explorer smoke tests only), `@types/three`, `gifenc` (root devDependency, pure-JS GIF encoder for `scripts/readme-media.ts` only; no ffmpeg needed).

## 3. Repo layout

```
architecture-visualization-cc-plugin/
  package.json  pnpm-workspace.yaml  tsconfig.base.json  biome.json  vitest.config.ts
  packages/core/       indexing library (no CLI, no MCP)
    src/paths/ config/ workspace/ discover/ parse/ lang/ hash/ resolve/ store/ heal/
        search/ embed/ describe/ dup/ advice/ adapters/ recipe/ review/ scip/ team/ types.ts index.ts
    scip/       precise mode (section 11): `proto.ts` (a protobuf wire reader: varints, length-delimited fields, skipping the rest),
                `read.ts` (`readScip`, a stream of the `Metadata` and then each `Document` of a SCIP index, with its occurrences and
                symbol information; no dependency, no generated code), `map.ts` (`mapScip`: documents and occurrences to symbols and edges).
    recipe/     (#203) `schema.ts` (zod `RecipeSchema`), `load.ts` (`loadRecipe`), `engine.ts` (ast-grep rules to emits to nodes),
                `data-access.ts` (#247: `table`, `read` and `write` emits, text rules and SQL text to tables and edges),
                `go.ts` and `go-routes.ts` (#368: Go rules, their examples, and Go routers, mounts and endpoints),
                `check.ts` (`gantry recipe check`), `nodes.ts` (the nodes it lists, #367), `helpers/` (`resolve-names`, `const-strings`, `mount-graph`, `call-return`,
                `param-flow`, `fs-routes`, `sql-text`, `text-rules`, `model-naming`, `method-classes`: one file each, generic;
                `fs-routes.ts` holds the file-system routing conventions)
    lang/       `LanguageModule` (id, extensions, workspace discovery, parser, resolver, token streams, embedding text,
                fact-sheet hints, kind labels, duplicate floors), `languageOfPath`, `lang/typescript/` (#220), `lang/engine/`,
                and `lang/packs/`. `lang/packs/<id>.ts` is a language pack: a typed constant (`LanguagePack`, `engine/pack.ts`)
                with `id`, `label`, `extensions`, `skipPaths` and `testPatterns` (globs), `generatedPatterns` (text) and `generatedPaths` (globs), `grammar` (the ast-grep
                language package and name), `nodes` (node kinds and the fields that hold name, parameters, result, body and receiver
                of functions and methods, type specs, types with a body of members (`types.bodied`: class, interface, enum, record, and the
                body kinds walked through), interface methods, constants, imports (with the separator of an import path, the kinds of
                a path that has no field, the named forms: one name, every name, static, and the statement forms that list modules or
                names of a module), call sites (with the field and kinds that
                say what is called), `typing` (optional: where the types sit, which makes the call resolver level 2, and, in `typing.flow`, what the
                flow resolver of level 3 reads), and the rules
                for `exported` (a capital letter, a modifier keyword, or a name without a leading underscore), signatures and
                measures), `workspace` (the manifests: the file names of each kind, or patterns that match them (`*.csproj`), whether it is read as text, XML, TOML or INI, the sources
                of the workspace name (a pattern, an element or attribute of an XML manifest, the folder, the name of the manifest file), where the dependencies are listed, and what makes an `app`; optionally the repo's group manifest and how two
                workspace names are the same), `tokens` (identifier, string, number and comment kinds, how locals are found), `kindLabels`, `factSheetHints`
                and `thresholds`; a test checks every pack against a zod schema. `lang/engine/` turns a pack into a `LanguageModule`
                (`buildLanguage`) and names no language: `grammar.ts`
                loads the grammars (one registration for all packs; a pack that names a registered grammar shares it) and wraps the
                node API, `walk.ts` the walk over the declarations of a file and the bodies of its types, `parse.ts` symbols and imports, `streams.ts`
                token streams and embedding text (the tree of the last file read is kept, one at a time), `workspace.ts` workspaces and
                their `depends_on` edges from the manifests (`xml.ts` reads an XML manifest by element paths and attributes, `config.ts` a TOML or INI one by the dotted paths of its keys), and `resolve/` the call resolvers (levels 1 to 3, section 7):
                `imports.ts` resolves an import path, `calls.ts` is the name resolver (level 1), `typed.ts` the type resolver
                (level 2; `read.ts` turns a file's tree into the plain data of `typing.ts`, `typeindex.ts` is the package index) and
                `flow.ts` the flow resolver (level 3, for a pack with `typing.flow`).
                `buildLanguage` gives a pack the type resolver when it has `nodes.typing` and the name resolver otherwise, and
                the flow resolver besides when it has `typing.flow`.
                `lang/packs/index.ts` lists the packs (`PACKS`); `Lang` and `LANG_IDS` are
                TypeScript plus the ids of `PACKS`. `lang/index.ts` lists the modules: TypeScript, then one `buildLanguage` module per
                pack in `PACKS` order. Go, Java, Python, C# and Rust have no code of their own: `lang/packs/go.ts`, `lang/packs/java.ts`, `lang/packs/python.ts`, `lang/packs/csharp.ts` and `lang/packs/rust.ts` are data, and their call, `extends` and
                `implements` edges (#222 for Go) come from the level 2 resolver (and Python's from level 3 besides); hash/ holds
                the language-blind half (`hashStreams` in `hash/shared.ts`, the only producer of the five hash values)
  packages/cli/        `gantry` binary (commander), hook entry points, git hook installer, shim
  packages/mcp/        MCP stdio server
  packages/explorer/   server/ (http for `gantry map`) and web/ (Vite + React)
  packages/protocol/   `@gantry/protocol`: the zod schemas, routes, limits and helpers of the server API and the upload format
                       (section 20), and a fake server for tests (`@gantry/protocol/testing`). Depends on `zod` only; the main
                       entry imports neither `node:http` nor `@gantry/core`; `@gantry/core` depends on it (the team client,
                       section 5), so no test of this package imports the core. `gantry team` is the only command that uses it
  action/              (#282) the GitHub Action users add to their own workflows: action.yml (composite), README.md,
                       post-failure.sh (the failure notice when the install failed), testdata/ (a review and
                       duplicates JSON pair from fixtures/pnpm-turbo and the comment snapshot),
                       src/ (comment.ts the comment body, github.ts the REST calls, run.ts the script, main.ts the entry
                       point; tsup bundles main.ts to plugin/dist/action.js, so the script ships in the release tarball and
                       no built JavaScript is committed here). Not CI for this repo (section 1)
  plugin/              Claude Code plugin directory (what gets installed)
    .claude-plugin/plugin.json  skills/  agents/  hooks/hooks.json  .mcp.json  bin/gantry  dist/
  fixtures/            synthetic repos for tests (see section 13)
  evals/               labelled-set scripts, seeded duplicates, recorded sessions, eval scripts (private data in evals/private/, git-ignored)
  docs/spikes/         M0 reports
  docs/milestones/     milestone reports
  scripts/pack.ts      release tarball writers (`packRelease` for the CLI, `packProtocol` for `@gantry/protocol`; `pnpm release:pack` runs both); scripts/release-deps/ holds the tarball's package.json and package-lock.json
  dist/                git-ignored release outputs: gantry-<version>.tgz, gantry-<version>.tgz.sha256, gantry-protocol-<version>.tgz and gantry-protocol-<version>.tgz.sha256
```

## 4. Paths and data

- Worktree root: `git rev-parse --show-toplevel`. `worktreeHash` = first 16 hex chars of sha256(realpath(root)).
- Data dir: one directory per worktree for every entry point (MCP server, hooks, skills, terminal CLI), `<base>/repos/<worktreeHash>/` (#141), or, for a repo that a workspace file lists, one directory per workspace, `<base>/workspaces/<first 16 hex chars of sha256(realpath(directory of the workspace file))>/` (#214, below). Every repo of a workspace resolves to the same data dir. `<base>` is, in order: `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` when set; else the plugin data dir derived from `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` when that is an installed plugin path `<claude home>/plugins/cache/<marketplace>/<plugin>/<version>` (base `<claude home>/plugins/data/<plugin>-<marketplace>`, characters other than letters, digits, `_`, `-` replaced by `-`; this is the directory Claude Code assigns); else `~/.gantry/data`, which only a run with no plugin base reaches (a terminal run from a clone). With a plugin base, nothing under `~/.gantry/data` is ever read or written, even when an index exists there; a plugin data dir without an index gets a fresh one, and `init` and a full `index` write `gantry: no index for this repository in <dataDir>; building a fresh one` to stderr first. Claude Code (plugins reference, checked in #141) exports `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT`, `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` and `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR` to hooks and MCP stdio servers, substitutes `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}` and `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA}` inline in skill, agent and command content, and does not put them in the environment of commands run through the Bash tool. So skills run `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA="${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA}" "${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}/bin/gantry" ...`, and `plugin/bin/gantry` exports `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` (its own parent directory) when it is unset, which covers a terminal run of the installed copy and the `bin/` entry on the Bash tool's `PATH`. **Who creates a data dir** (#147): `init`, `index`, `embed`, `map`, `config set`, `hooks install`, lookups (`search`, `node`, which heal first) and MCP tool calls (lazy refresh). Commands that only read or keep an index current (`backlog`, `usage`, `heal`), the SessionStart hook and the git hooks create nothing for a repository that has no store; they report the missing index instead. Before #147 `backlog`, `usage` and `heal` created an empty store there, which also hid the SessionStart reminder. A run from a clone path (`--plugin-dir`, `<clone>/plugin/bin/gantry`) has no derivable plugin base and uses `~/.gantry/data` unless `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` is set (docs/INSTALL.md). Contents: `gantry.db`, `overlay.db` (what a person decided: duplicate dismissals, saved node positions, labels, hidden parts, groups and saved views; attached to every store connection as `overlay`, section 9; tables are only ever added with `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, never dropped or altered; it has no schema version and Gantry never deletes it, only `gantry uninstall` does, with the rest of the data dir; `GantryPaths.overlayPath`), `parse-cache.db` (disposable per-file parse results keyed by file id (section 6) and content hash; a pure accelerator that is not part of the store schema and is safe to delete), `config.json`, `catalog/`, `logs/`, `models/` (embedding model cache may live in `~/.gantry/models/` instead, shared).
- Global config: `~/.gantry/config.json`. Shim: `~/.gantry/bin/gantry`.
- Team credentials: `~/.gantry/credentials.json` (directory mode 700, file mode 600, written atomically by `core/src/team/credentials.ts`), `{ "servers": { "<server URL>": { "token": string, "user": string, "expires_at": string } } }`, one entry per team server. `GANTRY_CREDENTIALS_FILE` moves it (tests). The token is never printed, logged or put in a config file, and nothing is written there until `gantry team login` signs in. When `GANTRY_TOKEN` is set the file is not read.
- Team cache: `<dataDir>/team-cache.db`, beside the index and never inside the repo (section 9, "Team cache schema"; `core/src/team/cache.ts`). It is the one local copy of team state: the MCP server and `gantry team status --pull` fill it from the server, and everything else only reads it. If it is deleted, the next pull rebuilds it.
- Recipe (#203): `<root>/.gantry/recipe.json`, a file of the target repo, not of the data dir. The user writes and commits it; Gantry never writes it. The main layer reads it from `HEAD` (`git show HEAD:.gantry/recipe.json`), the working layer from disk, review from each side's ref (section 11).
- `core/paths`: `resolvePaths(cwd: string, env?, opts?: { workspaceFile?: string }): GantryPaths` returning `{ root, repo, workspace, worktreeHash, dataDir, dbPath, overlayPath, configPath, catalogDir, logsDir, globalConfigPath, shimPath }`. `root` and `repo` are those of the repo the command runs in; `workspace` is `{ file: string | null, repos: { name: string, root: string }[] }` (#214): `file` is the workspace file (absolute) or `null`, and `repos` lists every repo of the workspace sorted by name (a repo with no workspace file is a workspace of one: `{ file: null, repos: [{ name: repo, root }] }`).
- **Workspace discovery (`core/src/workspace/index.ts`, `detectWorkspaces`).** The first rule that applies wins: `pnpm-workspace.yaml` `packages` globs; the root `package.json` `workspaces` globs; every directory holding a `project.json` (Nx); the projects the root `tsconfig.json` references; every directory holding a `package.json` when there is more than the root (a repo with no workspace tool); else only the root. The root workspace always exists. For tsconfig references, the root `tsconfig.json` is read as JSON with comments and trailing commas; each `references[].path` names a directory (its `tsconfig.json`) or a tsconfig file (its directory), and each referenced tsconfig's own `references` are followed the same way, every tsconfig read once, so cycles end. A path outside the repo, one inside `node_modules` or `.git`, one that names no existing tsconfig, and the root itself add no workspace. A `package.json` under a built-in exclude (section 5) or that git ignores adds no workspace in the last rule. A workspace without a `package.json` is named by its directory. A bare import of a workspace package name resolves to source: a `main`, `types` or `exports` target that is not itself a parsed file is mapped back to source. When the workspace has a `tsconfig.json` with `compilerOptions.outDir`, a path under `outDir` maps to the same relative path (`.d.ts` read as `.ts`, `.js` as `.ts`) under `rootDir`; with no `rootDir`, the root is the common directory of the static parts of `include` (the workspace directory when `include` is absent). Only a workspace without an `outDir` falls back to the names `dist`, `build`, `out` and `lib`, mapped to `src`.
- **Workspace file (#214; `core/src/workspace/file.ts`).** A project of several related repos lists them once in `gantry-workspace.json`, and one `gantry init` from any of them indexes all of them into one store. The file is `{ "repos": [{ "path": string, "repo"?: string }] }` (zod, strict, at least one entry). `path` is relative to the directory of the file, or absolute. A value that looks like a URL (`^[a-z][a-z0-9+.-]*://` or `^[^/]+@[^:]+:`) is rejected with `workspace file <file>: repos[<i>].path must be a local path, not a URL` (exit code 1); a path that is not the root of a git work tree is rejected with `workspace file <file>: repos[<i>].path <path> is not the root of a git repository`. `repo` overrides the repo name that `repoName` derives (it must not contain `:`). Two entries with the same name, or the same repo twice, are an error naming both entries. Remote repos are not supported.
- **Discovery (`findWorkspaceFile(repoRoot, { startAbove?, home? })`).** The walk starts at the parent of the directory the user is in (the path through which the repo was reached, so a repo reached through a symlink finds the file next to the link) and then at the parent of the real root, and goes up to the filesystem root, but not above the user's home directory when the walk passes through it. It takes the `gantry-workspace.json` that lists the repo (each `path` compared by real path with the repo's real root); a file above a repo it does not list is passed over, so the repo is a workspace of one. A repo that two files list is an error naming both (two indexes of one repo), which `--workspace` settles. A file met on the way that is invalid is an error (a file that cannot be read is not a file the repo can be sure it is not in). `--workspace <file>` on `init`, `index`, `heal` and `map` replaces discovery; the file must list the repo, else `workspace file <file>: does not list this repository (<root>)`. Hooks and the MCP server use discovery only, through `resolvePaths`. A linked worktree is its own repo with its own name rules: list it under its own `repo` name, or it stays a workspace of one.
- Rows of a repo that is no longer in the workspace file (or whose name changed) are deleted by the next full main index; the rest of the store, descriptions included, is kept. A repo that returns finds its descriptions (stored by content key) and the overlay (`overlay.db`) as they were, and a full main index copies the stored descriptions back into the search rows (`refreshDescriptionFts`), so `index` alone restores them.
- `GantryPaths.repo` is the repo name carried in every stable id (section 6), computed by `repoName(root)` (`core/src/paths`): the last two path segments of `git remote get-url origin` with any `.git` suffix and trailing slashes removed, as written (`https://github.com/a/b.git`, `git@github.com:a/b.git` and `ssh://git@host/a/b` all give `a/b`); with no `origin`, the basename of the worktree root (a linked worktree of a repo with an `origin` gives the origin name). A `:` in the name becomes `_`. Never a local path or `worktreeHash`, so two clones of one repo at different paths give byte-identical ids and `index --dump`.
- Nested worktrees under the repo (e.g. `.claude/worktrees/*`) are excluded from discovery of the parent.

## 5. Config (zod, `core/config`)

Project config (`<dataDir>/config.json`) is deep-merged over global (`~/.gantry/config.json`) over defaults.

```ts
type TaskName =
  | "describe.init"
  | "describe.sync"
  | "describe.precommit"
  | "describe.retry"
  | "overview"
  | "explain";
type GantryConfig = {
  precommit: { mode: "off" | "timeboxed" | "strict"; budgetMs: number; maxFunctions: number }; // off, 20000, 50
  routing: Record<TaskName, { model: "haiku" | "sonnet" | "opus"; effort: "low" | "medium" | "high" }>;
  exclude: string[]; // extra globs, added to built-ins
  workspaces: { overrides: Record<string, { kind?: "app" | "package" }> }; // keyed by workspace id (`ws:<repo>:<name>`), package name or directory
  duplicates: {
    mode: "warn" | "block";
    ignore: string[];
    thresholds: {
      nearJaccard: number;
      nearTokenJaccard: number;
      codeVector: number;
      codeVectorTokenJaccard: number;
      semantic: number;
    };
  }; // 0.85, 0.75, 0.98, 0.50, 0.60: fitted in M2 (#32, #131), see docs/milestones/M2.md; codeVector was 0.96 in M0; the two token gates were added in #131
  backlogReminderThreshold: number; // 100
  renameSuggestions: boolean; // false (measured in M0: Haiku flagged 0 of 23 misleading names, so precision is not measurable; stays false)
  worker?: { binary?: string }; // absent by default: absolute path of the `claude` binary for the headless worker; first in the binary resolution order (section 16)
  precise?: { scip?: string }; // absent by default (off): a SCIP index of the repo, a path relative to the repo's root or absolute; precise mode, section 11
  team?: { server: string; org: string; upload: { descriptions: boolean; working: boolean } }; // absent by default: no team. `server` is an https URL (http only for localhost and 127.0.0.1); `org` the organisation slug; `upload` is true and false by default
};
```
**Team config (`core/src/config/team.ts`, `core/src/team/`).** The `team` block lives in the project config file `<dataDir>/config.json`, which is per machine: nothing about a team is written into the repo's working tree or committed, and `gantry team link` writes it with the config writer. With no `team` block, no team variable and no sign-in, every command behaves as before and nothing contacts a server. Three environment variables override the file, so a pipeline needs no `team` block and no committed file: `GANTRY_SERVER` (the server URL), `GANTRY_ORG` (the organisation slug) and `GANTRY_TOKEN` (the bearer token, which is used for every request and is never read from or written to a file). `GANTRY_DEVICE_POLL_MS` replaces the polling interval of `gantry team login` (tests only). The client (`teamClient`, `core/src/team/client.ts`, the only code that calls `fetch` for a team server) is built from `ROUTES` in `@gantry/protocol`: it sends `Authorization`, `X-Gantry-Protocol` (the lower of `PROTOCOL_VERSION` and the server's maximum) and, for a token that does not start with `gct_`, `X-Gantry-Org`; it parses every answer with its tolerant schema; its failures are a `GantryError` (`core/src/errors.ts`) with a `code` of `team_unauthorized` (401), `team_forbidden` (403), `team_unreachable` (no answer within 5,000 ms), `team_protocol` (400 `protocol_version`) or `team_rejected` (any other error answer); and `ready()` calls `GET /v1/whoami` once per process to learn the server's maximum version, which `serverProtocol()` returns (1 until learned). `resolveTeamTarget(paths, config, env)` (`core/src/team/resolve.ts`) is what `gantry upload` uses to pick its server: `server` is `GANTRY_SERVER` when set, else `team.server`; `org` is `GANTRY_ORG` when set, else `team.org`; when either is missing it answers `null` and the command sends nothing. `token` is `GANTRY_TOKEN` (a CI token or a user token) when set, else the credentials file's token for that server, else `null`. It also returns the config to hand to the client, the given one with the `team` block's `server` and `org` replaced. Nothing is read from the repo's working tree. No hook imports the client, `resolve.ts` or the upload command: hooks never wait on the network.

**Precise mode config.** `precise.scip` names a SCIP index the user made with their own indexer; Gantry never runs an indexer or a toolchain. Every repo of a workspace reads it against its own root, so a workspace of several repos has one index file per repo at the same relative path, or an absolute path for a single repo. `gantry index --scip <file>` (a path relative to the current directory) names the index for that run, for the repo the command runs in, and wins over the config; it is not saved. With `precise.scip` set and the file missing, the index prints one stderr line (`gantry: SCIP index <path> not found; keeping inferred edges`, once per process) and keeps the inferred edges. A file that is not a SCIP index is an error.

Workspace file (#214, section 4): `gantry-workspace.json` is not part of `GantryConfig`; it is a file of the project, found by walking up from a repo, and `--workspace <file>` on `init`, `index`, `heal` and `map` names one explicitly. Config (`<dataDir>/config.json`, `exclude`, `workspaces.overrides`) lives in the workspace's data dir and applies to every repo of the workspace; each repo keeps its own git ignore rules and its own `.gantry/recipe.json`.

Boundaries (group `mayUse`, section 7) are warn-only: they have no `block` mode and no config switch, and nothing they print ever fails a hook, a commit or an edit (section 14).

Routing defaults: `describe.init|sync|precommit` haiku/low, `describe.retry` sonnet/low, `overview` sonnet/low, `explain` sonnet/medium.

**Recipe (`.gantry/recipe.json`, #203; `core/src/recipe/schema.ts`).** Where this repository declares routes, pages, endpoints, tables and the code that reads and writes them (#247), as data that every index applies unchanged. Nothing in it is evaluated as code, and every object is strict (an unknown key is an error).

```
{ "version": 1,
  "helpers": ["resolve-names" | "const-strings" | "mount-graph" | "call-return" | "param-flow" | "fs-routes"
              | "sql-text" | "text-rules" | "model-naming" | "method-classes"],
  "passthrough"?: ["get", "basePath", ...],
  "models"?: { "explicit"?: { "<model>": "<table>" }, "snakeCase"?: bool, "plural"?: bool },
  "methods"?: { "read"?: [method names], "write"?: [method names] },
  "tablePassthrough"?: ["alias"],
  "clientWrappers"?: [{ "symbol", "pathArg", "methodArg"?, "prefix"? }],
  "sql"?: { "tags"?: [...], "files"?: [globs], "exclude"?: [globs], "sqlFiles"?: [globs] },
  "rules": [{ "id", "language": "typescript", "when"?: { "dependency": [...], "viaWorkspaces"?: bool }, "files"?: [globs],
              "rule": <ast-grep rule>, "constraints"?: { "<META>": <ast-grep rule> },
              "emit": <emit>, "examples": { "match": [snippets], "noMatch": [snippets] } },
            { "id", "language": "go", "when"?: { "dependency": [Go module paths] }, "files"?: [globs], "uncalled"?: bool,
              "rule": <ast-grep rule>, "constraints"?: { "<META>": <ast-grep rule> },
              "emit": <go emit>, "examples": { "match": [snippets], "noMatch": [snippets] } },
            { "id", "language": "text", "when"?, "files": [globs], "rule": { "regex", "flags"? },
              "emit": { "kind": "table", "name": "$1" | ["$1", "$2"], "model"?: "$1", "data"? }
                    | { "kind": "query", "fn": "$1", "sql": "$2", "files"?: [globs] },
              "examples": { "match": [text], "noMatch": [text] } }] }
```

- `rule` and `constraints` are ast-grep rule objects handed to `findAll` unchanged; their shape is checked (known keys only). A rule runs on the files of the workspaces that declare one of `when.dependency` (the workspace's dependencies or frameworks) and that match `files` (picomatch, `dot: true`). Captures in an emit are `$NAME` (one node), `$$$NAME` (all nodes of a multiple metavariable) or `=text` (a literal, for a method).
- `emit` kinds: `router` (the match is a router instance; optional `base` path capture); `mount` (`parent`, `child`, optional `prefix`, optional `childIsScope` when the child is a function whose first parameter is the router, as in a Fastify plugin; `child` may be `$$$NAME` and mounts each router among its arguments); `endpoint` (`receiver`, `method`, `handler`, optional `path`, optional `pathFromChain`: when the path argument is absent or is no path, reuse the path of the previous registration in the same call chain and read every argument as a handler); `middleware` (`receiver`, `handler`, optional `path`, default `*`); `route` (a page route named by `id`, with `options` read for `component`, `loader`, `beforeLoad`, or `root`); `fileRoute` (a route whose id and URL come from the file's place in the routes folder by `convention`, now `tanstack-router`). Each emit may carry `data: { adapter }`, the label written into the node's `data.adapter` (for example `"hono"`); it is optional because TanStack Router route nodes carry none.
- Data-access emits (#247; no new node kinds, they write `table` nodes and `reads` / `writes` edges as section 9 lists): `table` (`name`: a capture, `=text`, `@class` (the class a matched decorator decorates) or a list of these, the first that reads as text; optional `model`: the name the code uses for the table (an entity, class or export name); optional `binding`: the identifier the match is assigned to stands for the table in later code; optional `data`: the node's data fields in the recipe's order, each `$X` (a capture's text, a string literal's value), `@name`, `@exportName`, `@symbol`, `@file`, `@line`, `{ "keysOf": "$X" }` (the property names of an object literal, or of the one a function returns) or a literal; `name` comes first unless the recipe places it); `read` and `write` (the table comes from `table`: an expression bound to a table, which is a string, an identifier, `ns.member` or a call named in `tablePassthrough`; from `model`: a model name, `$$$X` for several; or from `receiver`: the text of its declaration (`this.p` reads the enclosing class, `x.p` and `x` the declaration of `x`) is matched against the `receiverDecl` regexes, whose first group is the model and `{prop}` the member name; optional `method`: the match counts only when `methods` puts that method in this emit's class). A `read` or `write` with no table it can resolve emits nothing: a table is never guessed.
- **Go rules (#368).** A rule with `language: "go"` is an ast-grep rule over a `.go` file, run on the tree-sitter Go grammar the indexer already parses Go with (section 3, `lang/packs/go.ts`; no Go toolchain), so it has the same `rule`, `constraints`, `files`, `examples` and captures as a TypeScript rule. `when.dependency` names Go module paths: a workspace passes it when a `require` line of its `go.mod` names one. `gantry recipe check` wraps each example: a snippet that starts with `func`, `type`, `var`, `const` or `import` is a file body, anything else is a list of statements. **A pattern with one argument must give its context**: tree-sitter reads a bare `$R.Use($H)` as a type conversion, so it matches no call; write `{ "pattern": { "context": "func _() { $R.Use($H) }", "selector": "call_expression" } }` (patterns with two or more arguments, or `$$$`, parse as calls and need none). A rule matches the expression, not its statement: match `chi.NewRouter()`, not `r := chi.NewRouter()`.
  - Go emits are the TypeScript ones without the page routes (`route` and `fileRoute`), with these differences. `mount` without `child` mounts the matched expression itself under `parent` at `prefix` (a call that returns a new router, `parent.Group("/x")`); with `child` and `childIsScope`, the child is a function literal, or a named function, whose first parameter is the router (`r.Route("/x", func(r chi.Router) { ... })`). `endpoint` has `method` (a capture, `=ALL` or `=GET`; a name like `Get`, `GET` or `MethodGet` reads as `GET`) or `methodInPath: true`, which splits a leading `METHOD ` off the path text (`"GET /users/{id}"`) and gives `ALL` when the path has none; `pathFromChain` does not exist. `table` has no `binding`, `@class` or `keysOf`; its `name` is a capture or `=text`, and a capture that is a Go type reads as the type's name (`&articles.Article{}` gives `Article`), a string literal or a constant as its value. `read` and `write` have no `receiver` and `receiverDecl`: the table comes from `table` (a string, a constant, a concatenation of them; a name known only at run time is a `{<expression>}` table with `data.reason`, `low` edges) or `model` (a string, a type written out (`&User{}`, `[]User`), or any expression whose type the Go resolver infers: `x` after `var x User`, `x := &User{}`, a parameter, a field, a call's first result), and `method` as in a TypeScript rule.
  - Helpers on Go: `mount-graph` builds a full path from the prefix chain exactly as for TypeScript (router groups and sub-routers). `resolve-names` finds what a name stands for in the syntax of its file: the nearest `x :=` or `var x` before the use in the blocks around it, a parameter, an alias of a router (`v2 := v1`), a package-level `var`; `passthrough` lists chain methods that return the same router (`r.With(mw).Get(...)`). `const-strings` reads a string constant of the same file or of another file of the same directory (the same package), and `+` concatenations of strings and constants; an identifier or a `pkg.Name` it cannot read is a `{name}` piece, with `data.reason`. `call-return` follows a call to the function or method it resolves to (the Go resolver, which infers the type of a receiver such as `a.Account.router()` through struct fields and local assignments) and takes the router that function returns. `param-flow` makes a parameter a router when something passes one into it: the call is resolved to the function, the argument becomes a mount with no prefix. A function that takes a router and that no call in the read files names registers nothing by default. A Go rule may set `"uncalled": true` (#368, off by default, one flag per rule): the routes, mounts and middleware that rule finds on such a parameter are then registered at the paths the function writes (an unreachable `loadRoutes(mux)` still declares its routes). With the option on, dead code and callers outside the read set (test files are not read) count as "called nowhere", so the rule can list endpoints that never run. A function that is called, but whose call does not resolve, registers nothing in either case. `sql-text` reads Go string literals, raw strings and `+` concatenations whose other operands are constants (a name it cannot read becomes a placeholder, and a table named by it a run-time table); `sql.files` and `sql.exclude` globs apply to `.go` files too. `model-naming` and `method-classes` apply unchanged to the model a `model` capture yields. `fs-routes` and `tablePassthrough` do not apply to Go (no file routes, no `alias(...)` call), and `receiverDecl` does not exist for Go (a declaration's text is a TypeScript idea; use `model` with an inferred type). A field of a struct is not a router: `s.router.Get(...)` registers nothing unless a variable holds the router.
  - Attribution: a `read` or `write` belongs to the innermost Go function or method that contains the call, or to the file for package-level code (`var q = "SELECT ..."`); the edge is stored with that file. A `table` rule declares its node in the file that holds the match. A text rule `table` (a `.sql` file or a migration) belongs to the first extracted file of its workspace, in path order; a `.sql` file inside a Go module belongs to the module's workspace when the TypeScript workspace at the same directory has no file. A text rule with `emit.kind: "query"` (#368) says which Go function runs the statements of a text file: `fn` (a group) is a function name, or a method name without its type, and `sql` (a group) the text holding the statements. The tables that text reads (`FROM`, `JOIN`) and writes (`INSERT INTO`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE FROM`, `TRUNCATE`) become `reads` and `writes` edges from every function or method of that name in the Go files of the module that `files` (globs, default all) selects, to the tables that are declared; a table that is not declared is skipped, and a name with no function emits nothing. **Query-file attribution:** a text file has no row in the index (only files with a syntax tree do), so such an edge is stored with the Go file of the function (`fileId`, which also decides when the edge is replaced) and carries the text file as a second, structured field: `textFileId` (`text_file_id`, section 9), the id of the text file (`file:<repo>:<path>`). Its `evidence` entries are `<text file path>:<line>`, the line in the text file where the statement mentions the table (the line of the `FROM`, `JOIN`, `INSERT INTO`, `UPDATE` or `DELETE FROM`, counted from the start of the `sql` group), sorted by path then line; with several query files for one function and table, `textFileId` is the one whose path sorts first. There is no option: the query file, not the generated file, is where the access is written. `index --dump` has `textFileId` on those edges and no other. `trace` returns it on each traversed edge as `textFile` (the path), the table-usage view shows it on the edge (the hover text of the line), and `recipe check --nodes` lists the evidence lines. An edit or deletion of a query file re-indexes the Go files whose edges name it (now or before): PostToolUse and Stop treat a path that matches a `query` rule's `files` like a source file of the batch, and `heal` includes it in the changed paths. This is the shape of `sqlc` output, where each `-- name: GetAccount :one` block in a query file has a generated function of the same name; the engine knows no tool.
- A rule with `language: "text"` runs over files that have no syntax tree in the index (a schema file, SQL migrations), found with `git ls-files` (tracked, and untracked files that are not ignored; at most 1 MiB each) and matched by `files`; `rule.regex` is matched against the whole text (`flags` default `gm`) and `$1`, `$2` ... are its groups. A text rule emits tables, or (#368) queries, which attribute the tables a text reads and writes to a Go function (see Go rules). Only files with a syntax tree are rows of the index, so the node of a table declared in a text file belongs to the first TypeScript or Go file, in path order, of that file's workspace; `data.file` and `data.line` name the text file. A text rule's `when` is judged against the workspace of the text file.
- `clientWrappers` lists functions that make a request from their arguments where client-call inference does not see the flow (section 9, client wrappers): `symbol` is the function's symbol id (`sym:<repo>:<path>#<name>`), `pathArg` the index of the argument that is the request path, `methodArg` the index of the argument that is the method (default GET), `prefix` the text put before the path (`/api`). A listed function is a wrapper whether or not inference finds it, and every client call in its body is `wrapper_body`.
- `models` states how `model-naming` turns a model name into a table: the `explicit` map, then the name itself, then `snakeCase` and `plural` forms, and then the models the recipe's own emits declared. `methods` states which methods `method-classes` puts in each class; a method in both lists is a write, one in neither is no access. `sql` turns on `sql-text` (listed in `helpers`): the strings, template literals and tagged templates (`tags`, whatever they start with) of the TypeScript files in `files` minus `exclude` are read when they hold a statement shape (`SELECT ... FROM`, `INSERT INTO`, `UPDATE ... SET`, `DELETE FROM`, `CREATE TABLE`, `TRUNCATE`); `CREATE TABLE` declares a table, `FROM` and `JOIN` read, `INSERT INTO`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE FROM` and `TRUNCATE` write, names defined by `WITH` and catalogs with no declaration are skipped, and a substitution is followed to a constant or, with `call-return`, to the string a function returns. `sqlFiles` globs `.sql` files, which declare tables (a `.sql` file is not code that runs, so it has no reads or writes). `when.viaWorkspaces` widens a rule to workspaces that depend on a workspace of the repository that declares one of the `when.dependency` packages.
- **A recipe is untrusted input** (#247). The repository controls it, and it supplies JavaScript regular expressions (`rule.regex` of text rules and the `receiverDecl` templates), which backtrack: `(a+)+$` on a long line can run for hours. Limits, enforced by the schema and the engine: a pattern is at most 500 characters; a recipe has at most 50 text rules (the 51st and later are skipped with an error); one run reads at most 200 text files for text rules and 200 for `sql.sqlFiles`, each at most 1 MiB; a path that is a symbolic link, or that resolves outside the repository root (a linked directory), is never read. Over a cap is an error for that rule, which is skipped; nothing crashes. A recipe regex never runs on the calling thread: it runs in a `node:worker_threads` worker that the caller waits on for at most 100 ms per match call, 400 ms for all recipe regexes of one index run together (the worker start-up is not counted). When a budget is spent the worker is terminated, the rule is skipped for the rest of the run with one stderr line `Gantry: recipe rule <id> skipped: regex exceeded 100 ms` (an invalid regex gives `invalid regex: <reason>`), the rest of the recipe and the index complete, and `gantry recipe check` reports the rule as failed (exit 1). Hooks print that line to stderr only (their stdout stays JSON) and, with at most 400 ms of regex time, stay inside their 1,500 ms stop (section 14). Guaranteed: a recipe regex cannot hold the indexer, a hook or the MCP server longer than these budgets; a recipe cannot make Gantry read a file outside the repository through a text rule or `sqlFiles`. Not guaranteed: that a slow but finishing regex is cheap (it can use its whole budget on every run), that `gantry recipe check` finds a pattern that is slow only on a file it did not read (`receiverDecl` templates are tried on the rule's `match` examples), or anything about the `regex` keys of ast-grep rules (they run in ast-grep's Rust engine in linear time and are not limited here).
- A table whose name is known only at run time (`FROM ${table}` where `table` is a parameter, or an expression built in code) is a `table` node named `{<expression text>}` with `data.reason` (`table name is a function parameter`, `table name is built at runtime`), edges `low`: visible in the index, never a guess, and not scored (section 13).
- `passthrough` lists the chain methods that return the same router (`get`, `use`, `basePath`, `register`), so `new Hono().basePath("/v1").get("/", h).post(h2)` is one router. A registration counts only if its receiver resolves to a router a rule declared (a variable, an import that resolves to one, a router a factory function returns with `call-return`, or a function parameter that something mounts or passes a router into with `param-flow`), so `map.get(k)` is never an endpoint.
- Helpers: `resolve-names`, `const-strings` and `mount-graph` always run, and so do `text-rules` (when the recipe has a text rule), `model-naming` and `method-classes` (when the recipe states `models` or `methods`); `call-return`, `param-flow`, `fs-routes` and `sql-text` run when listed. They are generic and built on the adapter context's resolver and parse results; framework names appear only in a recipe, except the file-naming conventions of `fs-routes.ts`.
- Output is the adapter nodes of section 9 (`ep:`, `mw:`, `route:` ids; the data fields are the ones listed there), sorted, and the same recipe and tree give the same output. A route or endpoint whose path cannot be read keeps the unreadable piece as `{name}`, gains `data.reason` (`path is a function parameter`, `path is not a literal or const string`) and has `low` confidence on its edges.
- Loading (`loadRecipe(root, source)`, `source` one of `{ layer: "main" }`, `{ layer: "working" }`, `{ ref }`): a file that is not JSON or fails the top-level schema gives no recipe and one error; a rule that fails its own schema is dropped with an error naming its id and the zod message, and the rest run. With no recipe file the recipe adapter does nothing and Gantry prints nothing about it: not on `init`, `index` or a hook (the built-in adapters still map what they know). For a recipe that is present but invalid, `gantry init` and `gantry index` print one stderr line per problem: `Gantry: recipe rule <id> skipped: <reason>`, or `Gantry: recipe .gantry/recipe.json ignored: <reason>` when the file is not JSON or fails the top-level schema. Hooks print neither.
- **A text rule needs no helper entry** (#367). A recipe with a `text` rule reads its files whether or not `text-rules` is in `helpers`; the rule is the request, so listing the helper is allowed and changes nothing. Reason: recipes that listed it and recipes that did not looked the same to `gantry recipe check`, but only the first produced tables, and an error would have rejected recipes the check accepted yesterday while the removal rejects none. `method-classes` and `model-naming` were never gated by `helpers`: a `read` or `write` emit with a `method` uses the recipe's `methods` lists, and `models` is read when present. `sql-text` is different in kind: listing it is the switch that turns on scanning every TypeScript file (there is no rule to stand for it), so it stays opt-in. `call-return`, `param-flow` and `fs-routes` also stay opt-in.
- `gantry recipe check` (section 10) validates a recipe and runs every rule's examples. Zero matches in the repository is a warning, never a failure. With `--nodes` it also lists the nodes the index makes from the recipe (section 10).

**Bundler aliases (`core/resolve/bundlerAliases.ts`).** Imports that go through an alias defined only in a bundler config resolve to their source files. For each workspace, the resolver reads `vite.config.{ts,mts,js,mjs,cjs}` and `webpack.config.{js,cjs,mjs,ts}` in the workspace directory (not in parent directories). A config is parsed to a syntax tree with `oxc-parser` and never imported, required or run, so a config with side effects does nothing. The alias table is `resolve.alias` of the exported object: `export default { ... }`, `export default defineConfig({ ... })`, `module.exports = { ... }`, or the object an exported arrow or function returns (also inside `defineConfig(...)`). Both the object form (`{ key: value }`) and Vite's array form (`[{ find, replacement }]`, `find` a string) are read. A value is accepted when it is a string literal; `path.resolve` or `path.join` of a base (`__dirname`, `import.meta.dirname`, `process.cwd()`, all taken as the workspace directory) and string literals; `fileURLToPath(new URL("<literal>", import.meta.url))`; or a template literal made only of those parts. In a Vite config a string literal starting with `/` is relative to the workspace directory; elsewhere it must already be absolute. A key matches the specifier or the specifier followed by `/` and a subpath; a trailing `$` in a key (webpack) means the exact specifier only. The readable aliases go to an `oxc-resolver` of that workspace that has no tsconfig and is asked first for a specifier an alias key matches, so an alias wins over tsconfig `paths` and `node_modules` as it does in the bundler; when it finds nothing, the usual resolver (tsconfig `paths`, `node_modules`) runs. Anything else is not read: a computed value or key, a spread, a regular-expression `find`, a variable, an array of targets, or an `alias` that is not an object or an array. Such an alias is recorded as unreadable, and it changes only what the unresolved list says: a specifier that stays unresolved and starts with an unreadable key (or, when the whole `alias` value is unreadable or the key is not a string, any bare specifier that stays unresolved in that workspace) is an `UnresolvedSpecifier` with `reason: "alias_not_static"` and `alias` set to that key, or to `*`. `IndexResult.unresolved` carries both fields. Aliases of other build tools (Rollup, esbuild, Babel, Jest `moduleNameMapper`) are not read.

**tsconfig `extends` through a workspace package (`core/resolve/tsconfigChain.ts`).** Before the resolver of a workspace is built, the `extends` chain of the workspace's nearest `tsconfig.json` is read as JSON with comments (`extends` a string or an array). A relative `extends` resolves against the extending file (`.json` added when missing). A bare `extends` is tried through `node_modules` first, searching upward from the extending file; a file found there (a link to a workspace package included) is an ordinary link of the chain. Otherwise, a bare `extends` whose package name is a workspace resolves to that workspace's directory plus the subpath, through its `exports` map when it has one, else by path with `.json` added when missing. Any other bare `extends` is skipped and the rest of the chain still applies. The chain is merged as TypeScript does: `compilerOptions.paths` is replaced wholesale by a nearer config; `baseUrl` is inherited and resolved against the config that declares it; each `paths` target is relative to that `baseUrl`, or to the directory of the config that declares `paths` when the chain has none; `${configDir}` in `paths` and `baseUrl` is the directory of the leaf config. A base reached through two `extends` entries is read for each. When every link was found through `node_modules`, `oxc-resolver` gets the tsconfig as before. When a link was provided only by a workspace package, or by nothing, the resolver gets no tsconfig: the merged `paths` go in as absolute `alias` entries (`prefix/*` becomes the key `prefix`; a pattern with no `*` becomes an exact key ending in `$`; other pattern shapes are not mapped), longest key first, and the inherited `baseUrl` is added to the resolver's module directories. In this mode `references` and `rootDirs` are not applied.

**Sibling packages (`core/resolve/index.ts`, `sibling.ts`).** A workspace of several repos (section 4) is indexed with every repo's workspaces known before any repo is parsed. Resolution of a bare specifier goes in this order: `node:` and other scheme specifiers; a workspace package of the repo being indexed (its `exports` map, else `types`, `main`, `src/index`; built output under `outDir` maps back to source); a package that another repo of the workspace publishes, by the same rules, to that repo's file id (`file:<repo>:<path>`); then the bundler aliases and `oxc-resolver` with the `node_modules` lookup. A name that a package of the repo itself provides never goes to a sibling. A name that exactly one other repo provides resolves to its source, and a subpath its `exports` map does not list stays unresolved. A name that two or more other repos provide resolves to none of them: the specifier is classed `ambiguous` (counted in `specifierCounts.ambiguous`, reported as unresolved with `candidates`, the ids of the workspaces that publish it, sorted). The files of a sibling repo are parsed on demand from its work tree, only as far as the import leads (the entry file and the files it re-exports from); a sibling's own imports resolve by that repo's rules, one step deep. The edges are the usual `imports`, `calls`, `renders`, `uses_hook`, `exports` and type edges with their usual confidence, and every edge whose target is in another repo gets one more evidence entry, `sibling:<repo>`, after the `path:line` entries (so the cap of 20 lines does not drop it). A package that no repo of the workspace provides resolves as before.

**Built-in excludes:** `node_modules`, `dist`, `build`, `out`, `.next`, `.output`, `.turbo`, `.vercel`, `.wrangler`, `coverage`, `**/*.d.ts`, `**/*.gen.ts`, `**/routeTree.gen.ts`, nested worktrees, plus sensitive files `.env*`, `*.pem`, `*.key`, `*.p12`, `id_rsa*`, `.npmrc`, `credentials*`, `secrets*`, `*.tfvars`. Everything git ignores is excluded. Only `.ts .tsx .mts .cts` and `.go` are parsed. Go skip rules (#221): any file below a `vendor/` or `testdata/` folder or a dot folder is not indexed (the rules the Go tool applies); `_test.go` files are indexed and count as tests (`isTestPath`); a file whose header matches `^// Code generated .* DO NOT EDIT\.$` (multiline) is indexed with the file directive `generated` and the duplicate engine skips it, like `isGeneratedPath` files. The TypeScript built-in excludes above also apply to Go paths (a `build/` or `dist/` folder is skipped).

## 6. Stable IDs

- Workspace `ws:<repo>:<package name>` (e.g. `ws:acme/shop:@acme/ui`); root package `ws:<repo>:.`. A Go module (#221) is `ws:<repo>:<module path>` (e.g. `ws:acme/shop:example.com/shop/tools`); a repository with Go modules and no TypeScript has no `ws:<repo>:.`. The repo part is #214 (the spike behind it found the one id collision of two repos in one store, `ws:.`, which every repo has): a workspace id is built and parsed in `core/src/ids.ts` (`workspaceId`, `parseWorkspaceId`, `workspaceNameOfId`, `isRootWorkspaceId`).
- Every id carries the repo name (`GantryPaths.repo`, section 4) right after its prefix, so ids are the same on every machine and in CI. They are built and parsed only in `core/src/ids.ts` (`fileId`, `symId`, `routeId`, `endpointId`, `middlewareId`, `callId`, `tableId`, `parseId`, `pathOfFileId`, `pathOfSymId`, `nameOfSymId`, ...; the web app re-exports the same module as `explorer/web/src/ids.ts`). In the forms below `<repo>` is that name (it contains no `:`, but may contain `/`, as in `acme/shop`). `gantry node`, `gantry trace` and the MCP tools still accept the short forms a person types (a path, `path:line`, `file:<path>`, `sym:<path>#<name>`, `route:<ws>:<path>`, `table:<name>`) and resolve them against the current repo. A change of the repo name (for example a remote added later) changes every id; the next full index deletes the rows under the old name (`removeReposExcept`, section 9) and writes the new ones, so the store is not rebuilt and keeps its descriptions. The view-only `mwref:<package>:<name>` nodes of the endpoints view are not stored ids and carry no repo.
- File `file:<repo>:<repo-relative posix path>`
- Symbol `sym:<repo>:<path>#<name>`; class members `sym:<repo>:<path>#<Class>.<member>`; default export without a name `#default`; a second symbol with the same id in one file gets `~2`, `~3` by source order. Go (#221): a method is `#<Recv>.<name>` with the receiver's base type (`*` and type parameters stripped); a function declared in two build-constrained files (`listen.go`, `listen_unix.go`) has two ids, one per file, because the path is part of the id; `_` and `init` repeat within a package and are numbered `~2`, `~3` in source order, and the duplicate engine never compares them.
- Route `route:<repo>:<ws>:<path>` (`<ws>` is the workspace package name, the workspace id without `ws:<repo>:`; `<path>` is the TanStack route id, the string `createFileRoute` takes, such as `/posts/$postId` or `/_app/settings/`, and `__root__` for the root route); endpoint `ep:<repo>:<ws>:<METHOD> <path>` or `ep:<repo>:<ws>:rpc <procedure.path>` or `ep:<repo>:<ws>:fn <exportName>`; middleware `mw:<repo>:<ws>:<path pattern> <name>` (#45); table `table:<repo>:<name>`; unmatched client call `call:<repo>:<ws>:<label>` (#47; `<ws>` the caller's package, `<label>` what the call names: `rpc <path>`, `<METHOD> <path or URL>`, `<METHOD> <dynamic>`, `rpc <prefix>.*` for a sub-client alias, `useServerFn(<name>)`). In endpoint and middleware ids `<ws>` is a package name as for routes; for an HTTP route it is the package of the app at the top of the mount chain (the one serving the URL), for a server function the package of its file, for an oRPC procedure the package of the file of its root router (the router no other router nests; #46). `<procedure.path>` is the dotted key path from that root router. A table id is global: `<name>` is the SQL table name, and a second definition of the same name gets `~2` in file path order (#46). Adapter ids that repeat within a workspace get `~2`, `~3` in file path order, then source order.

## 7. Core types (`core/src/types.ts`)

```ts
export type Layer = "main" | "working";
export type Lang = "typescript" | (typeof PACKS)[number]["id"]; // every file and symbol carries one; TSX counts as typescript; the other ids are the language packs of `lang/packs/` ("go", "java")
export type SymbolKind =
  | "function"
  | "method"
  | "component"
  | "hook"
  | "class"
  | "type"
  | "interface"
  | "enum"
  | "const";
export interface SymbolRecord {
  id: string;
  fileId: string;
  name: string;
  kind: SymbolKind;
  lang: Lang; // language of the file
  exported: boolean;
  signature: string;
  startLine: number;
  endLine: number;
  loc: number;
  complexity: number;
  stmtCount: number;
  exactHash: string;
  renamedHash?: string; // exactHash with the symbol's own name replaced by `$0` (section 17, #151); absent on rows from before schema v6
  shapeHash: string;
  minhash: Uint32Array; // 64 permutations
  nearMinhash?: Uint32Array; // 64 permutations over the near-tier token stream (section 17, #131); absent on rows from before schema v5
}
export type EdgeKind =
  | "contains"
  | "imports"
  | "exports"
  | "calls"
  | "renders"
  | "uses_hook"
  | "extends"
  | "implements"
  | "references_type"
  | "depends_on"
  | "serves"
  | "handles"
  | "requests"
  | "reads" // function or file to table; written by the Drizzle adapter and by recipe data-access rules (#247, no new kinds)
  | "writes" // function or file to table; as `reads`
  | "similar_to";
export interface EdgeRecord {
  src: string;
  dst: string;
  kind: EdgeKind;
  confidence: "high" | "low";
  count: number;
  evidence: string[]; // `<path>:<line>` entries; for a `reads` / `writes` edge from a `query` text rule the path is the text file (section 5)
  fileId: string;
  level?: 1 | 2 | 3; // a `calls` edge from a language pack's resolver: the level that produced it (below)
  textFileId?: string; // a `reads` / `writes` edge from a `query` text rule: the id of the text file whose statement it is (section 5)
  source?: "scip"; // an edge written from a SCIP index (precise mode, section 11); absent on an inferred edge
}
export interface FactSheet {
  contentKey: string;
  id: string;
  path: string;
  kind: SymbolKind;
  language: string; // LanguageModule.label of the symbol's language: "TypeScript" | "Go" | "Java" (#224)
  signature: string;
  doc: string | null;
  callees: string[];
  returnType: string | null;
  runsWhen?: string; // host-written, only for a Go function named init (#372)
  body: string;
} // body trimmed to 1,500 chars
export interface Description {
  behavior: string;
  searchTerms: string[];
  nameFits: boolean;
  suggestedName?: string;
}
```
**Function-like kinds** (get descriptions, duplicate checks): function, method, component, hook. A component is a PascalCase function returning JSX; a hook is a function named `use[A-Z]…`.
**Go kinds (#221; `lang/engine/parse.ts` reading `lang/packs/go.ts`).** Only top-level declarations are symbols. `func` is `function`; a `func` with a receiver is `method`, named `<Recv>.<name>` after the receiver's base type; a `type` of a `struct_type` (or generic struct) is `class`, of an `interface_type` is `interface`, any other `type` spec or alias is `type`; every name of a `const` or `var` spec is `const` (the neutral kind set has no `var`). A spec lists its names in the grammar field `name`, which also holds the commas of `const a, b = 1, 2`, so only `identifier` nodes count. `exported` is an upper-case first letter. `signature` is the parameter and result text (`(key string) (string, bool)`; a method omits its receiver). `stmtCount` counts statement nodes in the body and `complexity` is 1 plus `if`, `for`, `case` clauses and `&&`/`||`. Display labels (`LanguageModule.kindLabels`): Go `class` is `struct`; `const` stays `const` for both `const` and `var` names, because the neutral kind does not tell them apart. Methods declared in the body of an in-repo interface are symbols too (#222): kind `method`, named `<Iface>.<Method>` (`sym:<repo>:<path>#Reader.Get`), `exported` by the method's first letter, `signature` as for any method, no body (`stmtCount` 0); an alias of an interface has none. Imports are kept with their path and optional alias; type references are not extracted.

**Call levels and the name resolver (`lang/engine/resolve/`).** A `calls` edge from the resolver of a language pack carries `level` (`edges.level`, section 9), which says which resolver produced it, and so what the edge claims: 1 the engine's name resolver (below), which claims that a name means the one thing it can mean, by the names alone: the function of the same file (`high`), or the only function of that name the caller can see (`low`); 2 the engine's type resolver (the next paragraph), which claims what declared types say, and also resolves names within a package, so every Go, Java, C# and Rust `calls` edge is level 2: the type a receiver is declared to have has the method (`high`, `low` for each of several overloads, none for a pack that does not guess them); 3 the engine's flow resolver (the paragraph after the next), which claims what the values assigned in the code suggest where no type is declared (a name assigned a constructor call, an attribute that an initializer assigns, the value a method is called on), and so every edge it makes is `low`, with no exception. The level is the resolver's, not a property of the confidence: a level 2 edge can be `low`, a level 3 edge never is `high`. `confidence` keeps its meaning at every level, and rankings that read confidence read it as before. Edges of other kinds and the `calls` edges of TypeScript carry no level (the field is absent in the dump). A pack with no `nodes.typing` gets level 1, which reads these pack fields: `nodes.call` (kinds of a call site), `nodes.callee` (`field` holding the called expression, or null when the call site itself holds the operand and the name, and then a member node without an operand is a bare name; `names` the kinds of a bare name, `member` the kind of `<operand>.<name>`, the fields of its two parts and the kinds an operand that is a plain name has) and `nodes.import.separator`. A call belongs to the innermost function or method that contains it; a call outside every function, and a call of the caller itself, give no edge. The callee is resolved by the first rule that applies: (1) a bare name goes to the function of that name declared in the same file (`high`), else to the only function or method of that name (the part of a symbol name after the last dot) among the symbols of the language the caller can see (`low`): those in the caller's workspace or in a workspace it depends on, never one in a test file (the pack's `testPatterns`) unless the caller is in a test file too, and a function and a method of one name clash and give no edge; (2) a name reached through an operand that is the local name of an import goes to the function of that name in the files the import names (`high`), and to nothing when the import is external; (3) a name reached through any other operand gives no edge: receivers belong to the levels that read types. A name with several candidates at the step that applies gives no edge, and the rules never fall through to a guess. Imports resolve from the import path and the workspace manifests: the path is a workspace name of the language followed, after the pack's separator, by the folder below that workspace's own; a folder with indexed files of the language in the same workspace, or in another workspace of the repo, is what the import names, and any other path is external. An edge's `count` is the number of calls and its `evidence` the `<path>:<line>` of each (capped like other edges). The resolver satisfies the `Resolver` interface (`targetAt` returns the callee a call or a bare or member name at a source range resolves to; `typeNameAt` is always null at level 1), so adapters and the indexer do not change.

**Level 2, declared types (`lang/engine/resolve/typed.ts`, `typeindex.ts`, `read.ts`).** A pack that has `nodes.typing` (`TypingRules`, `engine/pack.ts`) gets this resolver in place of level 1, and no rule of level 1 is a fallback for it: a call whose receiver type is unknown gives no edge. The block says where the types sit, as node kinds and grammar fields only (a test checks that it holds no function): how a type expression reads (plain names, a name qualified by an import, wrappers such as pointers, generic instantiations, sequences with their element field, maps, and the names the language itself provides), the package clause (and the ending that makes a package of its own that no import reaches), imports that bind no name, the shape of a list of result types, the members of a class type (an entry without a name is an embedded or extended type), the elements of an interface body that name another interface, the field of a declaration's type parameters, package-level variables, function names that are never called, the forms of a callee (bare names, members and the kinds an operand has), a conversion written as a call, the functions the language provides whose result type the call site gives, how an expression reads (wrappers, names, an element of a value, expressions that have the type of their operand, expressions that have a type they name), where a name declared in a function gets its type (parameters, declarations with an optional type and values, ranges, and names whose type is not read) with where each becomes visible, and the kinds of expression a place in a file can name; and, each only for a pack that has it: `construction` (what a call of a type of the repo runs: the name of its initializer method, the names of methods that create the value themselves and the names of types outside the repo whose subclasses are created some other way), `parent` (the call that stands for the one base of the enclosing type), `decorators` (where decorators sit and which of them mark a function as an overload stub), `type.nullable` (generics and binary expressions that read as their one type argument or their one operand that is not the absent value) and `type.quoted` (a type written as a string), `supertypes[].keywords` (entries of a supertype list that are options of the declaration, not supertypes) and `package.reexports` (for a pack whose packages are files: how many index files a name imported from one is followed through). A file is parsed by the indexer for its symbols; the type resolver then parses it again, once for its declarations (the package index is built from them over every file of the language, on the first call) and once more for the function bodies when the edges of that file are asked for, and drops each tree as soon as it has turned it into plain data (`typing.ts`: a type as written, an expression as far as its type is read, the names a function declares, its call sites, and the declarations of the file). The package index, the scopes and the edges are computed from that data, so nothing but one tree is alive. `targetAt` and `typeNameAt` keep the tree of the last file they were asked about, with the expressions found in it and the scopes of the functions looked in, and replace it when another file is asked for (as the token streams do): the many lookups of one file, such as one per route, parse it once; level 1 does the same for `targetAt`. The cost is therefore two extra parses of every file of the language on the first index run and one more per lookup run of a file, against holding every tree. Per function the type environment is the declared names with their types (scopes flattened); a method is looked up on the receiver's type, then through its embedded or extended types; a call on a value of an interface type goes to the interface's method symbol (the declaring interface's when the method comes from an embedded one). `calls` edges are `high`, except where the number of arguments leaves several overloads of one name: each is then a `low` edge (and `targetAt` gives none), or, for a pack whose `typing.arity.overloads` is `none`, no edge at all; `extends` runs from a class to each in-repo type it embeds or names as a supertype (`high`); `implements` runs from a type to each in-repo interface whose method names it covers (`low`), or, for a pack whose `typing.implements` is `nominal`, to each in-repo interface its declaration names (`high`). A call of a type of the repo, for a pack with `construction`, is an edge (`high`) to the initializer the type declares, else to the one its nearest base declares, found through bases that are each the only one; it makes no edge when a class on the way lists several bases and declares none, when the chain leaves the repo or ends before one is found, or when the type or any type of the repo among its ancestors is created some other way (a decorator, a keyword option among its supertypes, a method that creates the value, a base the pack lists as special). A method called on `parent` is looked up the same way in the one base: the method it declares, else the one its only base declares or inherits, and no edge with several bases, a base outside the repo, a method declared twice, a class on the way that is created some other way and does not declare the method, or when any type of the repo that lists several bases has the enclosing type among its ancestors (the value may be of that type, and which base is meant then depends on the order its bases are searched in). A function declared several times in one file, where exactly one is not marked by a decorator the pack lists as an overload mark, is that one for every call; any other repetition has no target, and so has a method that a type body declares twice, for a pack that does not tell overloads apart by their arguments (`arity`). A name imported from an index file of the repo that binds it with an import of its own is the name it is bound to there, followed through at most `package.reexports` index files; a name the index file declares itself or binds twice is not followed, and no name is followed from an index file that has a star import (it may rebind any name). A call through an interface is not also given an edge to each implementing method: that would add edges the measured truth does not have and lower the precision the bars require.
The block also carries what a language with types in bodies needs, each of them optional and chosen by the pack: `nodes.types.bodied` and `bodies` (declarations that are types with a body, and the body kinds walked through: a method in a body is named `<type>.<method>` after the chain of types around it, `Outer.Inner.m`, and a type in a body `Outer.Inner`); `typing.members.declarator` (a field entry names its fields through declarators), `typing.supertypes` (the fields, or the kinds when the grammar has no field, of a type declaration that name its supertypes, each with the edge it makes), `typing.enclosing` (a bare name or call inside a body names a member of the enclosing type, of a type it extends, or of a type around it;
