a local never hides a method; `this` and `super` kinds; a name that is a type reaches its static members), `typing.arity` (overloads are told apart by the number of parameters and arguments; a variadic parameter takes any number from its position less one), `typing.construct` (an expression that calls a constructor of the type it names: the constructor is the method named like the type, not inherited), `typing.package.path` (`clause`: a package is the name its declaration gives, whatever the folder; `directory`: the workspace and folder), `nodes.import.named` (an import names one type, or every type of a package with a marker child, or, with a `static` child, a member of a type or every member of it; one name is bound in the file, the others are looked up by package), `typing.locals.inferred` (a declared type that stands for the type of the value) and `parentType` (a declarator that takes the type of its parent); `typing.type.generic.arguments` and `typing.type.parameters` (a type keeps its type arguments, and the type parameters of a declaration, also a method's own through the `generics` field of its rule, stand for them in its fields, results and supertypes; an unbound parameter is unknown); `typing.type.library` (types outside the repo, by package, whose listed methods return one of their type arguments); `nodes.types.anonymous` and `constants` (bodies of members that no type declaration holds: their methods are symbols named after the body, `<type>.<member>.<prefix><n>` or `<type>.<constant>`, marked `anonymous`, and the body is a type of the index that extends the type created; its methods see the names declared in the member it was created in). With `typing.enclosing`, code of a type body that is outside every method has the symbol of the type as its caller. A method of an in-repo type is found on the type, then on each supertype in order of the declaration, and an interface's method symbol is the target of a call through the interface. A symbol a call refers to is found by the offset of its declaration, so overloads, which the symbol ids tell apart with `~2`, `~3`, each have their own target.
A language whose packages are files and whose imports are statements adds, optional and chosen by the pack: `typing.package.path` `file` (each file is a package of its own, named by its path below its workspace without the folders `typing.package.roots` names and without the extension, the file named `typing.package.index` standing for its folder; an import names a package by that path, looked for in the workspace of the importing file and then in each workspace it depends on, a relative one from the package of the file, in its workspace; the key of a package carries the workspace name, so two workspaces with a package of one path stay apart); `nodes.import.modules` (statements that list modules, `import a.b as c, d`, or names of a module, `from m import x as y, *`, found at the top of a file and under the statement kinds `through` lists, such as the branches of a condition; a name that is a package of the repo is the package, and an import without an alias binds the first or the last segment of its path, as `binds` says); `typing.deferred` (the kind of a child of a function that makes its result a deferred value, which is not the declared type: such a function has no result type); `locals.nameLists` (the kinds that hold a list of names) and an empty field name (the names are the children of the node); `locals.untyped[].scope` (a name is visible from the start of the node around it); and a name the file imports by name is, at a bare call, a function or a type of the repo. Without `typing.enclosing` a bare name never means a member of the enclosing type, so a name in a class body is no field of it. A pack's `workspace.sameName` says when two workspace names are the same (case and separators), so a dependency entry finds its workspace, and its manifests may be TOML or INI (`config.ts`), read by the dotted path of a key or the keys of a table.
A language whose types sit in namespaces, may be written in several parts and may get methods from outside their type adds, optional and chosen by the pack: `typing.package.clause` (the kinds of the declarations that name the package of a file, with `name` the field that holds the name; a file with more than one such declaration, or one inside another, has no package of its own and is read as a file with none) and `package.nested` (the types of a package are in scope in each package whose name starts with its name followed by the import separator: `A.B` sees `A`); `typing.type.qualified.packages` (a qualifier that is the whole name of a package a file declares names that package, `A.B.T`, besides a name an import binds); `nodes.import.named.bare` (what an import without an alias and without the on-demand marker brings in: `name`, its last segment, or `demand`, every name of its path; with an alias it is the last segment under that name) and an alias, which is not the path (the on-demand marker `onDemand` is optional); `nodes.types.partial` (a bodied declaration with a modifier of that text is one part of a type: the parts of one name in one package are one type, with their members, fields and supertypes together, each part read in its own file, a type having a part that is not marked is not merged, and each part has the symbol of its file and its own `extends` and `implements` edges); `typing.supertypes[].edge` `inferred` (one list holds the base class and the interfaces: `implements` from a class to an in-repo interface it lists, `extends` for any other pair); `typing.type.byParameterCount` (the number of type parameters is part of the identity of a type: a name that a package declares with different numbers, `Foo` and `Foo<T>`, is several types that a use site is not matched to, so it is no type of the package, with no member of it a target, and it hides the same name of a package further out; parts of a partial type merge only when their numbers are equal); `typing.type.builtin` (names of types the language provides, each a type of its own that no file declares); `typing.extensions` (a method whose first parameter has the marker child is a method of the type that parameter has: `x.m(a)` that no method of the type of `x`, or of its supertypes, answers goes to the one in a package the file sees (its own, those it is nested in, those it imports whole) that has the name, takes one argument fewer than its parameters, and has a first parameter of the type of `x` or of one of its supertypes in the repo; when an extension of the name that fits the number of arguments has a first parameter that is not read (a type parameter, a type with type arguments), which one the call means is not known and it has no edge, none is read for a type of the repo that has a supertype outside it, nor for a call on a type name, and a type outside the repo is taken to have no method of that name); `typing.arity.rest` (the field of a parameter list that holds parameters written without a node of their own, which take any number of arguments), `optional` (the kind of the token that starts a default value: such a parameter may be left out) and `overloads` (`low`: each overload that fits is a `low` edge; `none`: the call has no edge when more than one fits, because the types of the arguments would tell them apart); `typing.enclosing.callableLocals` (a local, a function or a delegate, may be called, and a call of its name is then no call of a method); `typing.members.holder` (an entry whose names are declarators held by a child of that kind: `int a, b;` has one type and one declarator for each name; any other child of it is no name); `typing.locals.declarations[].values` absent (the value is the last child of the declaration that is no name); the field of type parameters may be the kind of the child that holds them (`generics`); the name of a member that is an instantiation (`m<T>()`) is the name it instantiates; `nodes.exported` `modifier` with `node` the kind of each child that holds modifiers (a declaration may have several) and `owners` (the kinds of the declarations whose members are exported without a modifier); and a manifest named by a pattern (`workspace.manifests[].files`), by the name of its file (`name: { fileName: true }`) or by an attribute of an XML element (`<element path>@<attribute>`), whose `depends_on` edge belongs to the manifest of its source workspace.

A language whose methods sit in blocks apart from their type, whose module paths start with a word and whose imports are trees adds, optional and chosen by the pack: `nodes.types.impls` (a block of members of a type declared elsewhere: the fields of its type, of the interface it names and of its body, how the name of the type is found in the type expression (`baseType`), and the field of the block's own type parameters; its members are symbols named `<type>.<member>`, as those of a type body are, and a block whose type is one of its own type parameters, or is not read, names the type `baseType.unknown` and has no type around its code: `self` and the name for the type around it stand for nothing there; a method of such a block whose type another package declares is also a method of that type there, a type that has no declaration in the repo has no method found, and a block written for a reference to a type, or for any other wrapper of one, is a block of no type of the repo; the interface a block names is a supertype of its type wherever the block is: an `implements` edge (`high`) from the type to the interface, and a method the interface declares with a body is found on the type); `typing.type.fielded` (kinds that wrap a type and hold it in a field: a reference, an object type, an opaque type) and `typing.type.self` (the name that stands for the type of the declaration whose body the code is in); `typing.self` (the kinds of the expression that stands for the value a method is called on, without the rest of `typing.enclosing`: a bare name never means a method); `typing.type.parameters.bare` and `bounds` (a type parameter with exactly one bound, written with it or in the clause the pack names, reads in the parameters and locals of the declaration as the type of that bound, never in a result type; a parameter with no bound or with several, and a bound whose kind is in `ignore`, is not read, and a name that is a type parameter is no type of the repo of that name); `typing.package.entries` (the first of these file names that a workspace has directly in its root folder stands for the workspace itself, as an index file stands for its folder) and `package.paths` (a module path starts with the word for the root of the workspace, for the module of the file or for the module above it, once for each level up, or else with the name of a workspace, the file's own or one it depends on, or with the name of a module inside the module of the file; a name an import or a module declaration bound is that module, and a type qualified by such a path is found through it, and through the re-exports of `package.reexports`); `nodes.import.trees` (an import written as a tree of paths with lists, renames, a word for the prefix itself and an every-name marker, read as one import for each name from the path of its prefix) and `nodes.import.submodules` (a declaration of a module without a body is an import of that module from the module around it, and one with a body is no import); `typing.callee.paths` (a name reached through a path of names: the prefix names a module of the repo, a type of the repo or the type the name for the type around stands for, and the name is a function of that module or a method of that type; a prefix that starts with a type parameter, or names anything else, gives no edge) and `typing.callee.operandsAreValues` (the name in front of the operand of a member is always a value: an import that binds a module, and a type of that name, are not read there); and, under `typing.locals`, a kind listed under `untyped` hides the names it binds from a function of that name, a function declared in a block is known in the whole block (`scope`), and the function the names are collected for is not one of its own names; `locals.blocks` (a `let` is known to the end of the nearest block around it of a listed kind, and an outer name of that spelling is known again after it; a name declared by any other kind is known to the end of the function) and `locals.items` (the kinds of the items declared in the body of a function, with the field of each name, and, with `imports`, the names that an import in a body brings: each hides, in the whole function, the type, function and module of that name that the module declares or imports, and no edge is made for a call through it); `nodes.types.impls.nested` (the blocks in a module written in place add their methods to the type of that name, which is the last name of a qualified one, though no symbol stands for them, so a method that such a block also declares is ambiguous); and, for `typing.package.entries`, a workspace with two entry files has two crates: the entry that stands for the workspace is its root, another entry is the root of its own crate, which only that file knows (`crate::` in it is the file, and `super::` in it nothing), and `crate::` or a `super::` that would reach the root from any other file of such a workspace names no module.

**Level 3, flow (`lang/engine/resolve/flow.ts`).** A pack whose `typing` block has a `flow` block (`FlowRules`; node kinds, fields and names only, a test checks that it holds no function) gets this resolver besides the type resolver. It stands on the same package index and reads the same names, plus the values that reach a name or an attribute that has no declared type, and it makes an edge only for a call site that rests on such a value: every edge it makes is `low` and has `level: 3`, with no exception, and every other call site is the type resolver's. A pair of symbols that the type resolver already links keeps its level 2 edge. The facts it reads are: a declaration written without a type (`flow.untyped` lists the kinds: `x = Foo()`) gives its name, from its end on in the function, the type of its value, when the value is a call that names a type of the repo (a call whose callee names a type creates a value of that type only for this resolver; at level 2 such a call has no type, and its only edge is the one to the initializer that `construction` gives), the call of a function with a declared result type, or a name that has a type; a variable of the top of a file assigned that way has the type in every function of the file, unless the file assigns it twice; the parameter a method names `flow.self` is the type whose body holds the method; and an assignment to an attribute of that parameter (`flow.attribute`) in the method of the type that `construction.initializer` names (`self.repo = Repo()`, `self.repo = repo` with `repo: Repo`) types the attribute for every method of the type and of the types that extend it. An attribute whose assignments in the initializer give different types, or one that is not known, has no type; an attribute that only another method assigns has none either. A call on the parameter or on an attribute is looked up in the type, then in its supertypes in the order of the declaration, as at level 2, and a method that a subtype overrides is no edge. Nothing is guessed for a name without such a value: a parameter without a type, a name a loop or a `with` binds, a name assigned a value that is not read. A name whose nearest declaration before the call is such a flow fact has a type only when every declaration of that name in the function (parameters, annotated and unannotated assignments, on any path) gives the same type; otherwise its type is unknown and the call has no edge. A declaration without a type hides the annotated one before it from the type resolver, which reads nothing of it.
The language sections below say what a pack fills.

**Go call edges (#222; level 2 reading `nodes.typing` of `lang/packs/go.ts`; syntax and local type inference, no Go toolchain).** Its `calls` edges are level 2. The resolver builds a package index over the parsed `.go` files: per package path (the module path of the `go.mod` workspace holding the file plus the folder below it; an external test package `x_test` is a package of its own that nothing imports) the functions with their result types, the types with struct fields, embedded fields and interface method sets, the methods by receiver base type and the package-level vars; when two files declare one name (build-constrained files) the first path in sorted order wins. An import path inside a workspace maps to its package; the default import name is the target's `package` clause, never the last path element; an import outside the repo makes no edge, and dot and blank imports are ignored. Per top-level function or method, names are collected with their types with scopes flattened (the nearest preceding declaration wins; a declaration is visible after its own statement; function literals count as the function around them): receiver, parameters, named results, `var x T`, `x := T{}`, `&T{}`, `new(T)`, `make(T, ...)`, `append(s, ...)`, `x := F()`, `x, err := pkg.F()`, `x := y.M()` (declared result types), `x.(T)`, `s[i]`, struct field chains through embedding, aliases, and range variables over `[]T`, `[N]T`, `map[K]T` and named types of those. Edges are in the section 7 kinds, with no new kind: `calls` from the caller's symbol to the callee's at `high` confidence for a same-package call, a package-qualified call and a call of a method on a concrete type (declared on it or promoted through struct embedding); a call on a value of an interface type goes to the interface's method symbol `#<Iface>.<Method>` at `high` (the symbol of the interface that declares the method when it comes from an embedded in-repo interface). A site whose receiver type is unknown, a method of an external type (`http.ResponseWriter.Header()`), a function-valued field or local, a builtin and a conversion emit nothing; a call of a function by itself emits no edge. `extends` runs from a struct to each in-repo type it embeds (`high`). `implements` runs from a named in-repo, non-generic type that is not an interface to an in-repo, non-generic interface when its method names (value or pointer receiver, promoted ones included) cover the interface's method set, at `low` confidence: names only, no signatures; an interface that embeds one from outside the repo, or a constraint element, is skipped, and an interface with no method gets none. `evidence` is `path:line` of the call, embedded field or type declaration. Measured (V2 spike, `docs/spikes/v2-go-calls.md`) against go/packages truth by `evals/go/score-calls.ts`; the bars are in the fixtures section. Go imports are not stored as `imports` edges, and `resolveSpecifier` has nothing to resolve for Go. A Go module's file `contains` edges and `depends_on` edges between modules (a `require` naming another module of the repo; with a root `go.work`, only modules it lists with `use`) are stored on the requiring module's `go.mod` file row.

**Java kinds (`lang/engine/parse.ts` reading `lang/packs/java.ts`; tree-sitter Java, no JDK).** Symbols are declarations at the top of a file and in the bodies of types, found by the walk of `lang/engine/walk.ts`. A `class_declaration` or `record_declaration` is `class`, an `interface_declaration` or `annotation_type_declaration` is `interface`, an `enum_declaration` is `enum`. A type nested in another is named by the chain of types around it (`Outer.Inner`, `sym:<repo>:<path>#Outer.Inner`). A method, a constructor and a compact record constructor in a body are `method`, named `<type>.<name>` after the type whose body holds them (`Outer.Inner.ping`; a constructor repeats the type's name, `App.App`). Overloads share a name: the repeated names of one file get `~2`, `~3` in source order, as always. The methods in the body of an anonymous class (`new T() { ... }`, `types.anonymous`) and of an enum constant (`RED { ... }`, `types.constants`) are `method` symbols too; the body itself is no symbol. An anonymous body is named after the member that holds the expression, `<type>.<member>.$<n>`: `<type>` is the named type, or the body, whose member it is, `<member>` the name of the method, of the field whose declarator holds the expression or of the enum constant whose arguments do, and `n` counts from 1 in source order within that member (`Outer.make.$1.write`, `Gson.doubleAdapter.$1.write`, `TypeAdapters.BYTE.$1.read`; one inside another body is named in that body: `Outer.make.$1.run.$1.run`). A method that repeats a name of its type carries the `~2`, `~3` of its own symbol (`Outer.make~2.$1.write`), and a body in a member without a name (an initializer block) is `<type>.$<n>`, counted over such members of the type. An enum constant's body is named `<enum>.<constant>` (`Color.RED.describe`). These ids are stable as long as the type around, the member's name and the order of the anonymous bodies inside that one member stay the same: adding an anonymous body renumbers only the ones after it in the same member. An anonymous body inside a class declared in a method has no symbols, as that class has none. Every symbol declared inside an anonymous or enum-constant body carries `anonymous: true` (`symbols.anonymous`, section 9), so a view can leave them out; no view does yet. Fields are not symbols; they are read for their types only. `exported` is the `public` modifier, or a member of an interface body (public without a modifier). `signature` is the parameter list and then the type written before the name (`(String name) String`; a constructor has no type). `stmtCount` counts statement nodes in the body and `complexity` is 1 plus `if`, `for`, enhanced `for`, `while`, `do`, `case` groups and rules, `catch`, `?:` and `&&`/`||`. A file is generated when its path matches `**/generated/**` or `**/generated-sources/**` or it holds an `@Generated` annotation (`generatedPaths`, `generatedPatterns`); the directive is the same `generated` as Go's. A file's imports are kept as `single` (`import a.b.C;`: local `C`, imported `C`, specifier `a.b`), `on demand` (`import a.b.*;`: local and imported `*`, specifier `a.b`) and `static` (`import static a.b.C.m;`, and `.*`: specifier `a.b.C`). Display labels: `const` is `constant`. Skipped paths: `target/`, `build/`, `.gradle/`; test files: `src/test/**`, `*Test.java`, `*IT.java`.

**Java workspaces (`lang/engine/workspace.ts`).** A workspace is a folder with a `pom.xml` (named by the project's own `<artifactId>`, not its parent's; its dependencies are the `<artifactId>` of each `<dependencies>` entry of the project, not of `dependencyManagement`), or with a `build.gradle` or `build.gradle.kts` (named by `rootProject.name` of a `settings.gradle` or `settings.gradle.kts` in the same folder, else by the folder; its dependencies are the projects it names with `project(":a")` or `project(":a:b")`, by the last segment). A folder with both is a Maven workspace. A name that a `settings` file gives to a project of another folder (`include` with a changed `projectDir`) is not read. A workspace is an `app` when a source file that is not a test declares `static void main(`. `depends_on` edges join the workspaces of the repo whose names a manifest lists; Java has no group manifest, so every workspace of the repo is a sibling, and the edge belongs to the requiring workspace's manifest file (`pom.xml`, `build.gradle`).

**Java call edges (level 2 reading `nodes.typing` of `lang/packs/java.ts`; syntax and local type inference, no JDK).** The resolver builds a package index over the parsed `.java` files: a package is the name of the file's `package` declaration (a file without one is in the package of its workspace and folder), not the folder; per package the types (nested ones as `Outer.Inner`) with their fields and supertypes, and the methods by the type whose body holds them, each overload with its number of parameters (a `...` parameter takes any number from its position less one). A name written in a file means, in this order: a type parameter of the method it is in, of a method around an anonymous body it is in, or of a type around it (no type of the repo: nothing is known of it, even when the repo has a type of that name); a type nested in a type around it; a type the file imports by name (it hides a type of the package with the same name, so `import java.util.Optional;` in a package that declares its own `Optional` means the JDK's); a type of the package; a type of a package imported with `.*`; anything else is a type outside the repo and gives no edge. Names are collected per method as for Go (flattened scopes, the nearest preceding declaration wins): parameters, typed locals, `var` locals typed by their value (`var h = new H()`), `for (T x : xs)`, resources, and, outside the method, the fields of the type and of the types around it and inherited from its supertypes. `this` is the enclosing type and `super` its superclass. A bare call `f(...)` is looked up in the enclosing type (its own methods, then its supertypes in the order of the declaration), then in each type around it, then in the static imports (`import static a.B.f;` and `import static a.B.*;`); `T.f(...)` with `T` a type of the repo is a static call; `x.f(...)` goes through the type of `x`; `new T(...)` goes to the constructor of `T` that fits and is not inherited (none when `T` declares none). The overloads whose parameter count fits the call are the candidates; one candidate is a `high` edge, several are one `low` edge each, none is no edge. `calls` edges are level 2. `extends` runs from a class to its superclass and from an interface to each interface it extends, `implements` from a class, enum or record to each interface it names: `high`, from the declaration, with the line as evidence, for in-repo types only; nothing is guessed from method names. A call through an interface type goes to the interface's method symbol (`Greeter.greet`); a call is not also given an edge to each implementing method. A site whose receiver type is unknown, a method of a type outside the repo (the JDK and libraries: `String`, `System.out`), a lambda parameter, a record component accessor, a qualified type name (`Outer.Inner x`, `a.b.C x`: such a type is not read), a method reference (`this::handle`, `Type::method`) are given no edge. **Type arguments.** A type keeps the arguments written after it (`List<Order>`, `Box<Order>`, `Map<String, Order>`; a diamond `<>` and a raw type have none, a wildcard is an unknown argument). For a generic type of the repo the type parameters of its declaration (`class Box<T>`) stand for the arguments wherever its fields, the result types of its methods and its supertypes name them, so `box.get().total()` with `Box<Order> box` and `T get()` reaches `Order.total`, also through `class OrderBox extends Box<Order>` and through a result such as `Map<K, V>`. A type parameter that is not bound (a raw type, a parameter of the method itself such as `<T> T pick()`, a parameter of the class the code is in) is unknown, and a call on it gives no edge; nothing is inferred from arguments, and an `X.class` literal is not read. For types outside the repo the pack lists, as data (`typing.type.library`), the JDK containers and which type argument their accessors return: the `java.util` collections, iterators and `Optional` (`get`, `getFirst`, `peek`, `poll`, `pop`, `next`, `orElse` and the like return argument 0), the maps (`get`, `getOrDefault`, `put`, `computeIfAbsent` and the like return argument 1, the `...Key` accessors argument 0), `java.util.concurrent` maps and futures, `Supplier` and `AtomicReference`. Such a type is known by its package, so only where the file imports it by name or imports its package with `.*`; a type of that name declared in the repo, imported from another library or not imported at all is not one. A call of a listed accessor makes no edge itself (its target is outside the repo); it only gives the type the next call is looked up on, and a method that is not listed gives no type. **Callers outside methods.** A call in a field initializer, in a static or instance initializer block or in the arguments of an enum constant has the enclosing type's symbol as its caller (`calls` from the class, enum or interface, level 2), typed as code of that type. A call in the body of an anonymous class or of an enum constant belongs to the method of that body that holds it (the symbols above); one in an initializer of such a body belongs to the method the body was created in, or to the type when it was created in an initializer. Such a body is a type of the index that extends the type created (the enum, for a constant's body): its `this` and bare names find its own methods first, then what it extends, and the types around it only when every supertype is in the repo. A method of such a body also sees the parameters and locals of the member the body was created in (and of the members around that one, for a body in a body), declared before the body: such a captured name comes before the fields of the types around, and after a field of that name that the body itself declares or inherits from a type of the repo. It makes no `extends` edge. A call in the body of a class declared inside a method (and of an anonymous class inside one) is attributed to the method around it, but its `this` and its bare names are typed by what the body extends (the type created, or the declared supertypes), never by the class around the method: a name the body declares itself gives no edge (it has no symbol), a name found on a supertype of the repo gives that edge, and the type around is searched only when every supertype of the body is in the repo. In the same way a bare name that a class does not declare gives no edge when any type in its chain of supertypes is outside the repo (the name may be inherited), and the types around it and the static imports are not searched.

**Python kinds (`lang/engine/parse.ts` reading `lang/packs/python.ts`; tree-sitter Python, no interpreter).** Symbols are the function and class definitions at the top of a file and in the bodies of classes, found by the walk of `lang/engine/walk.ts`, which goes through a decorated definition (the symbol starts at the definition, not at its decorators) and through the statements of a class body. A function at the top of a file is `function`, a class is `class`, a function in a class body is `method` named `<class>.<name>` and a class in a class body `Outer.Inner`; `async def` is the same node with an `async` child and changes nothing; a repeated name (a property and its setter) gets `~2`, `~3` in source order, as always. A function or class inside a function, anything under `if` or `try` at the top of a file and every assignment are not symbols: a module variable or an attribute is read for its type only. `exported` is a name without a leading underscore (`__init__` is not). `signature` is the parameter list and then the annotation after `->` (`(self, repo: Repo) None`). `stmtCount` counts statement nodes in the body and `complexity` is 1 plus `if`, `elif`, `for`, `while`, `except`, `case`, conditional expressions, the `for` of a comprehension and `and`/`or`. A file is generated when its path matches `**/*_pb2.py` or `**/*_pb2_grpc.py` (`generatedPaths`; the directive is the same `generated` as Go's). A file's imports are kept as `import a.b.c` (local `a`, specifier `a`: the statement brings in the first segment), `import a.b as c` (local `c`, specifier `a.b`), `from m import x as y` (local `y`, imported `x`, specifier `m`), `from . import x` and `from ..m import x` (the specifier keeps its leading dots) and `from m import *` (local and imported `*`); an import under `if`, `else`, `try` or `except` at the top of a file counts, one inside a function does not. Display labels: `const` is `constant`. Skipped paths: `.venv/`, `venv/`, `__pycache__/`, `site-packages/`, `build/`, `dist/`; test files: `tests/**`, `test_*.py`, `*_test.py`; extensions `.py` and `.pyi`.

**Python workspaces (`lang/engine/workspace.ts`, `lang/engine/config.ts`).** A workspace is a folder with a manifest that gives a name, tried in this order: a `pyproject.toml` with `[project] name`, a `pyproject.toml` with `[tool.poetry] name`, a `setup.cfg` with `[metadata] name`, a `setup.py` with a `name=` string (so a `pyproject.toml` that only configures tools does not hide the name of a `setup.py`). Its dependencies are the names in `[project] dependencies` (each requirement string is cut where its name ends: `core>=1.0 ; python_version > "3.9"` is `core`), the keys of `[tool.poetry.dependencies]`, the lines of `[options] install_requires` of `setup.cfg` and the strings of `install_requires=[...]` of `setup.py`. Two names are the same when they are after lower-casing and replacing each run of `-`, `_` and `.` by `-` (`workspace.sameName`): `my_lib`, `my-lib` and `My.Lib`. `depends_on` edges join the workspaces of the repo whose names a manifest lists; Python has no group manifest, so every workspace of the repo is a sibling, and the edge belongs to the requiring workspace's manifest file. A workspace is an `app` when a source file that is not a test holds `if __name__ == "__main__":`. A file belongs to the workspace with the deepest folder that holds it, and a repo with no manifest that names a project has no Python workspace: its `.py` files are indexed for their symbols but make no call edge. TOML and INI are read by `config.ts`, which has no dependency: the strings a key holds by its dotted path (a string, or the strings of an array; a number, a boolean and an inline table hold none) and the key names of each table.

**Python call edges (levels 2 and 3 reading `nodes.typing` of `lang/packs/python.ts`; syntax and local flow, no interpreter).** A package is a module: its path is that of the file below its workspace's folder, or below `src/` when the file is there, without the extension, and `__init__.py` is its folder (`src/acme/service.py` is `acme.service`, `src/acme/__init__.py` is `acme`). An import names a module by that path, in the workspace of the importing file and then in each workspace it depends on, never in another; a relative import (`from . import x`, `from ..m import f`) is read from the package of the importing file and stays in its workspace. A name written in a file means, in this order: a function, class or variable of the module; a name the file imports by name (`from m import f, Foo as G`); a module (`import m`, `import a.b as x`, and `from a import m` when `a.m` is a module of the repo). A module outside the repo, a builtin and a name that is bound in a way that is not read give no edge. A name imported from a package (`from pkg import f`) whose `__init__.py` binds it with an import of its own (`from .mod import f`, also `from .mod import g as f`) is that name of the module it is imported from there, through at most three `__init__.py` files; nothing is followed from an `__init__.py` that has a star import, an `__all__` is not read, and a name the `__init__.py` declares itself or binds twice (in a `try` and its `except`) is not followed, and a name found nowhere gives no edge. A function or method that one file declares twice (a redefinition, a property and its setter) has no one meaning and is not a target, unless all but one of the declarations carry `@overload` (also `typing.overload` and the like): then it is the one without, the implementation, and a call reaches it (the stubs are symbols of their own, `f`, `f~2`, and the implementation is `f~3`); stubs with no implementation have no target. A `.pyi` stub next to its `.py` file does not replace the function of the module. An annotation is read where it is a plain name, a name qualified by a module (`models.User`) or a generic (`list[User]`, whose arguments are kept); `Optional[A]` (also `typing.Optional[A]`), `A | None` and `None | A` read as `A`; a string (a forward reference) reads as the name inside when that is a plain or qualified name (`"A"`, `"models.A"`), also inside `Optional[...]`; a union of two classes (`A | B`), `Union[A, None]`, `Optional[A | B]`, any other string and a subscripted base class (`Base[T]`) are not read, so a name annotated with one has no type. **Level 2, declared types:** a parameter or a name assigned with an annotation (`x: A = ...`) has the annotation's type, so has a variable annotated at the top of a file, and an annotated name in a class body is an attribute with that type; the result of a function is its `->` annotation, except that of an `async def` (its result is a coroutine, so `await f()` gives no type either); a call `x.m()` is looked up in the type of `x`, then in its bases in the order they are written. `f()` for a function of the module, `mod.f()` for a function of an imported module and `f()` for a function imported by name need no type: each is a level 2 edge, `high`, as a package-qualified or same-package call is for Go, because the name is bound by a declaration or an import and nothing is guessed. A call through a base type goes to the method that type declares or inherits and is not also an edge to each method that overrides it. `Foo(...)` for a class of the repo (a name of the module, a name imported by name, or `mod.Foo()`) is a level 2 edge, `high`, to `Foo.__init__` when the class declares it, else to the `__init__` of its nearest base, found through bases that are each the only one: no edge when a class on the way lists several bases and declares none, when no class of the chain declares one, or when the chain reaches a base outside the repo first. No edge either when `Foo`, or any class of the repo among its ancestors, is created some other way: it carries a decorator (`@dataclass` included), has a keyword among its bases (`metaclass=M`), declares `__new__`, or has `Enum`, `IntEnum`, `StrEnum`, `Flag`, `IntFlag`, `NamedTuple` or `TypedDict` among its bases. `super().m()` with no arguments, in a method of a class that lists exactly one base, which is a class of the repo, is a level 2 edge, `high`, to the method `m` that base declares, else to the one its only base declares or inherits (also `super().__init__()`); no edge for a class with several bases, with a base outside the repo or with none, for `super(Cls, self)`, outside a method, or when a class on the way lists several bases and declares no `m`, declares `m` twice, or is created some other way (a decorator, a keyword among its bases) and does not declare `m`; and none when any class of the repo that lists several bases has the enclosing class among its ancestors, because the instance may be of that class and the order its bases are searched in is not read. **Level 3, flow, always `low`:** `x = Foo()` and then `x.m()` in the function (the name is assigned a call of a class of the repo, and every assignment to that name in the function gives the same class: `if c: x = Foo()` and `else: x = Bar()` leave `x` with no type and the call with no edge), a variable of the top of a file assigned a class call and used in a function, `self.m()` where the method is declared in the class or inherited from a base of the repo, and `self.attr.m()` where every assignment to `self.attr` in `__init__` gives the same known type (`self.attr = Foo()`, or `self.attr = attr` with `attr: Foo`). `self` is the parameter named `self` of a method. A call on `self.attr` that no assignment in `__init__` types gives no edge. Nothing is guessed for a parameter without an annotation, a loop, comprehension, `with`, `except` or walrus variable, a lambda parameter, a name declared again with a value that is not read or an attribute that `__init__` does not assign. A name that a parameter, a loop, a comprehension, a `with`, a lambda or a nested function declares hides a name of the same spelling at the top of the file. A base class that the repo does not hold ends the search for a method that was not found before it. `extends` runs from a class to each base it names that is a class of the repo (`high`, from the declaration); `implements` edges are never made. A call outside every function (module level, class body) has no caller symbol and gives no edge. `evidence` is `path:line` of the call.

**C# kinds (`lang/engine/parse.ts` reading `lang/packs/csharp.ts`; tree-sitter C#, no .NET SDK).** Symbols are declarations at the top of a file, inside namespaces (written as a block or file-scoped; no name has the namespace in it) and in the bodies of types, also those inside every branch of a conditional block (`#if`, `#elif`, `#else`: all branches are read, so a member declared in two branches has the repeated-name suffixes), found by the walk of `lang/engine/walk.ts`. A `class_declaration`, `struct_declaration` or `record_declaration` is `class`, an `interface_declaration` is `interface`, an `enum_declaration` is `enum`. A type nested in another is named by the chain of types around it (`Outer.Inner`). A method and a constructor in a body are `method`, named `<type>.<name>` after the type whose body holds them (`Outer.Inner.Ping`; a constructor repeats the type's name, `Report.Report`); the repeated names of one file (overloads) get `~2`, `~3` in source order, as always. Each part of a partial type is a symbol of the file that holds it, with the methods of that file. Fields, properties, indexers, operators, finalizers, events, delegates, local functions and the accessors of a property are not symbols: the engine names a symbol after the `name` field of its declaration and walks the members of a type body, and an accessor has the name `get` or `set` and sits inside a property, which is read for its type. The code of an accessor, of an expression-bodied property and of a field initializer belongs to the symbol of the type around it, and the code of a local function to the method around it. `exported` is a `public` modifier (a declaration has one node per modifier), or being a member of an interface. `signature` is the parameter list and then the type written before the name (`(string name) string`; a constructor has no type). `stmtCount` counts statement nodes in the body and `complexity` is 1 plus `if`, `for`, `foreach`, `while`, `do`, `case` sections, switch expression arms, `catch`, `?:` and `&&`/`||`. A file is generated when its path matches `**/*.g.cs` or `**/*.Designer.cs` (`generatedPaths`; the directive is the same `generated` as Go's). A file's imports are the `using` directives at the top of the file (also `global using`; one inside a namespace block is not read) and are kept as `using A.B;` (local and imported `*`, specifier `A.B`: every type of the namespace), `using static A.B.T;` (`*`, specifier `A.B.T`: every member of the type) and `using X = A.B.T;` (local `X`, imported `T`, specifier `A.B`); an alias of a constructed type (`using X = A.B.List<int>;`) names no type of the repo. Display labels: `const` is `constant`. Skipped paths: `bin/`, `obj/`; test files: `*Tests.cs`, `*.Tests/**`; extension `.cs`.

**C# workspaces (`lang/engine/workspace.ts`, `lang/engine/xml.ts`).** A workspace is a folder with a `*.csproj`, named by the `<AssemblyName>` of the project, else by the name of the project file without its extension (a folder with several is the workspace of the first, in the order of the names, that gives a name). Its dependencies are the project files its `<ProjectReference Include="...">` entries name, each by its file name without `.csproj` (a slash or a backslash separates the folders), so a reference finds the workspace that is named by its file name, and not one that sets another assembly name. A workspace is an `app` when a source file that is not a test declares `static void Main(`, `static int Main(`, `static Task Main(`, `static Task<int> Main(` (also with `async`); top-level statements make no app. `depends_on` edges join the workspaces of the repo whose names a project file lists; C# has no group manifest, so every workspace of the repo is a sibling, and the edge belongs to the requiring workspace's `.csproj`. A file belongs to the workspace with the deepest folder that holds it; `bin/`, `obj/` and `node_modules/` are not searched for projects.

**C# call edges (level 2 reading `nodes.typing` of `lang/packs/csharp.ts`; syntax and local type inference, no .NET SDK).** The resolver builds a package index over the parsed `.cs` files: a package is the namespace of the file's one `namespace` declaration (block or file-scoped), not the folder; a file without one is in the package of its workspace and folder, and so is a file with several namespace declarations or with one inside another (it has no namespace of its own). The types of one namespace are one package across the files, and across the projects of the repo. A type written in a file means, in this order: a type parameter of the method or of a type around it (no type of the repo: nothing is known of it); a type nested in a type around it; a type the file names with an alias (`using X = A.B.T;`); a type of the namespace of the file; a type of each namespace the file is nested in (`namespace A.B;` sees the types of `A`); a type of a namespace imported with `using A.B;`; anything else is a type outside the repo and gives no edge. `A.B.T` names the type `T` of the namespace `A.B` when a file declares that namespace (a qualifier that is no declared namespace gives no type, so `Outer.Inner` written outside `Outer` gives none); the keyword types (`string`, `int`, `object`, ...) are types of their own, outside the repo. Names are collected per method as for Java: parameters, typed locals, `var` locals typed by `new T(...)`, a cast `(T)x`, `x as T` or the declared result type of a called method of the repo, `foreach (T x in xs)`, `catch (T e)`, a pattern `is T x` and `out T x`; the elements of an array and a nullable type read as the type they hold. Fields and properties are members with the type they declare, also those inherited from a supertype in the repo; the fields of a declaration with several names (`T a, b;`) each have the type. A name that a lambda parameter, a deconstruction, the name of a property pattern, an `out var`, a query range or a local function declares hides a field of that spelling and has no type. `this` is the enclosing type and `base` the base class, but a call on `base` gives no edge. A bare call `f(...)` is looked up in the enclosing type (its own methods, then its supertypes in the order of the base list), then in each type around it, then in the types a `using static` names; `T.f(...)` with `T` a type of the repo is a static call; `x.f(...)` goes through the type of `x`; `new T(...)` goes to the constructor of `T` that fits and is not inherited (none when `T` declares none); a constructor initializer (`: base(...)`, `: this(...)`) is no call site. The overloads whose parameter count fits the call are the candidates (a parameter with a default value may be left out, a `params` parameter takes any number from its position less one); one candidate is a `high` edge, and several are no edge at all, because the types of the arguments would tell them apart and are not read. A call through an interface type goes to the interface's method symbol (the declaring interface's when the method comes from an interface it extends); it is not also given an edge to each implementing method. A part of a partial type sees the fields, methods and supertypes of every part. An extension method (`static R M(this T x, ...)`) is called as `x.M(...)`, found as described under the abilities above: it is looked for in the namespaces the file sees (its own, those it is nested in, those it imports whole; a `using static` does not make an extension visible), for a receiver of the type `T` or of a type that has `T` among its supertypes in the repo, and a call on a variable of the keyword type `string` finds an extension of `string`. A method of the type, or inherited from a supertype of it, wins over an extension method of that name, and `Exts.M(x)` is an ordinary static call. Code outside methods (a field initializer, a property accessor, an expression-bodied property) has the symbol of the type as its caller; a lambda or local function belongs to the method around it. `calls` edges are level 2. `extends` runs from a class to its base class and from an interface to each interface it extends, `implements` from a class, struct or record to each interface it lists: `high`, from the declaration, with the line as evidence, for in-repo types only. A site whose receiver type is unknown (`var x = Unknown();`), a method of a type outside the repo (the BCL and libraries), a conditional access (`x?.M()`), a delegate or local function called by its name, the result of `await` (not read: `var r = await M();` has no type), a generic type parameter, a tuple, a `dynamic` value, an indexer, a target-typed `new()`, a call that an extension method of a receiver not read (`this IEnumerable<T> x`, `this TBuilder b`) could mean, a type name that the package declares with and without type parameters (`Foo` and `Foo<T>`: neither the type nor its methods are a target) and a call on `base` are given no edge.

**Rust kinds (`lang/engine/parse.ts` reading `lang/packs/rust.ts`; tree-sitter Rust, no Cargo).** Symbols are the `fn` items at the top of a file (`function`), `struct` items (`class`), `enum` items (`enum`) and `trait` items (`interface`), and the `fn` items in the body of a trait (also one that has no body: `method`, named `<Trait>.<name>`) and in an `impl` block (`impl T { }` or `impl I for T { }`: `method`, named `<T>.<name>` after the type of the block, written without its type arguments: `Circle<i32>` is `Circle`; a block whose type is one of its own type parameters (`impl<T: A> B for T`), a reference (`impl B for &T`) or any other form that is not a plain or generic type name is `?`). The methods of one name that several blocks declare (an inherent one and one of a trait, or the same block under two `#[cfg]`) are repeated names of one file and get `~2`, `~3` in source order; the symbol of a block is none. Items of a module written in place (`mod a { ... }`, which includes `#[cfg(test)] mod tests { ... }`) are not read: the names in it are not those of the file. Constants, statics, type aliases, unions, `macro_rules!` macros and functions declared inside a function are not symbols. `exported` is `pub` (also `pub(crate)`) or being a method of a trait; `signature` is the parameter list, then the result type (`(&self) f64`); `stmtCount` counts expression statements, `let`s, constants and statics, and `complexity` is 1 plus `if`, `for`, `while`, `loop`, match arms and `&&`/`||`. A file is generated when it starts with `// @generated` (the directive is the same `generated` as Go's). A file's imports are the `use` declarations at the top of the file (not those inside a function or a module written in place; `pub use` too), one import for each name: `use a::b;` (local `b`, imported `b`, specifier `a`), `use a::b as c;` (local `c`), `use a::{b, c as d, e::f, self, *};` (each name from the path of its prefix; `self` is the module of the prefix, so `use a::b::{self}` is `use a::b`; a glob is `*` with the prefix as specifier), `use foo;` (specifier empty); a leading `::` is dropped. `mod a;` is the import of `a` with the specifier `self`: the module of a file of its own. Display labels: `class` is `struct`, `interface` is `trait`, `const` is `constant`. Skipped paths: `target/`; test files: `tests/**`, `*_test.rs`, `tests.rs` (a file that a `#[cfg(test)] mod x;` declares is not found by its content); extension `.rs`.

**Rust workspaces (`lang/engine/workspace.ts`, `lang/engine/config.ts`).** A workspace is a folder with a `Cargo.toml` that has `[package] name`, named by it; a manifest with only `[workspace]` names no crate and makes no workspace. Its dependencies are the keys of its `[dependencies]` table (not those of `[dev-dependencies]` or `[build-dependencies]`), and `-` and `_` make one name (`workspace.sameName`), so `acme-core` is found by `acme_core`. A workspace is an `app` when a source file that is not a test declares `fn main(` (also `pub` or `async`). `depends_on` edges join the workspaces of the repo whose names a manifest lists; the `members` of a `[workspace]` are not read, so every manifest with a package in the repo is a crate of the repo, and the edge belongs to the requiring workspace's `Cargo.toml`. A file belongs to the workspace with the deepest folder that holds it; `target/` and `node_modules/` are not searched for manifests.

**Rust call edges (level 2 reading `nodes.typing` of `lang/packs/rust.ts`; syntax and local type inference, no Cargo).** A package is a module: a file is the module of its path below its workspace's folder, or below `src/` when it is there, without the extension; `mod.rs` stands for its folder, and `lib.rs`, else `main.rs`, directly in that folder for the crate (`typing.package.entries`; a `main.rs` beside a `lib.rs` is the module `main`, which refers to the library by its crate name). The module tree is that of the files, not of the `mod` declarations, and `#[path]` is not read. A module path is read as `typing.package.paths` says: `crate::` is the root of the workspace, `self::` the module of the file, `super::` the module above it (repeated for each level), the name of a crate (the workspace itself or one it depends on, `-` and `_` the same) is its root, and any other first name is a module declared by `mod a;` in the module of the file, a name a `use` or a `mod` bound, or a module inside the module of the file. A `use` brings in a name by its path (also through groups, `self`, renames and `pub use` re-exports, followed through three levels of `lib.rs` and `mod.rs` files), a glob brings in nothing (a name that two globs could supply, or one, gives no edge), and a `use` inside a function brings nothing, but hides, in that function, the name it brings (a type, function or module of that name of the module is not looked up there, and neither is one that a `struct`, `enum`, `trait`, `type`, `mod`, `const` or `static` declared in the function brings), so a call through such a name has no edge. A `let` is known to the end of its block: `let x = A::new(); { let x = B::new(); } x.m()` goes to `A.m`. The blocks of members in a module written in place (`mod inner { impl super::S { .. } }`) are read for their methods only, which have no symbol: a method name that such a block and another declare for a type is ambiguous and has no edge. With a `lib.rs` and a `main.rs` in one folder, `crate::` in `main.rs` is `main.rs`, and `crate::` or a `super::` that reaches the root in any other file of the package has no edge. A type written in a file means, in this order: a type parameter of the function or block around it (no type of the repo of that name; with exactly one bound, the type of that bound, in the parameters and locals of the function: `fn f<T: Shape>(x: T)`, `where T: Shape`; with several bounds, or one written twice, none), `Self` (the type of the `impl` block, or the trait, around: none when the block's type is not read), a type of the module, a name a `use` binds, a path (`a::T`, `crate::a::T`, `super::T`) with its re-exports; anything else is a type outside the repo. A reference, `dyn T` and `impl T` read as `T`; `Box`, `Rc`, `Arc`, `Option` and the other types of the standard library and of other crates are types outside the repo, and so is a method called on them: no edge. Names are collected per function: parameters, `let` with a type, with a call or a struct literal on the right, `&x`, and a `Type::new()` whose declared result is read (`Self` in it is the type of the block); every name a pattern binds (a `let` with a pattern, `for`, `if let`, a `match` arm, a closure parameter) hides a function of that name, and a function declared in a block is known in the whole block. A struct is a type with fields (the types of its named fields), a tuple struct has no field read, and a call of a tuple struct by its name has the type, an enum variant (`E::A(1)`) none. A method call `x.m()` goes through the type of `x`: the method of its blocks (also one of `impl Trait for Type`), else the method the trait it implements declares with a body; `self.m()` is the type of the block around; `Type::f()`, `Self::f()` and `module::Type::f()` are a function of the type, `module::f()` a function of the module, `f()` one of the file's own module or one it names in a `use`. A trait method called on a value whose type is a trait (`&dyn T`, `impl T`, a parameter bound by `T`) goes to the trait's method symbol (also in a trait's own default method on `self`), and not also to each implementer; a method that the trait inherits from a supertrait goes to the supertrait's. The number of arguments leaves out the receiver and tells methods of one name apart (`typing.arity`, `overloads: none`): a name that both an inherent block and a trait block of the type declare, that two traits declare, or that several blocks declare under different `#[cfg]`, gives no edge. A trait implemented in another module or file than its type is a supertype of the type (its blocks' methods are the type's: the blocks are looked for wherever they are in the crates of the repo). `calls` edges are `high` and level 2; `implements` runs from a type to each in-repo trait an `impl Trait for Type` block names, `high`, from the block's line, and `extends` from a trait to each in-repo trait it names as a supertrait. The arguments of a macro are not expanded (the body of `println!(..)`, `vec![..]`, `assert_eq!(..)` is a token tree, so no call in it is read), and a macro invocation is no call: `macro_rules!` macros are not symbols, so a call of one has no edge, and no name brought by a macro or by `#[derive]` (`S::default()`, `s.clone()`) is a function of the repo. A site whose receiver type is unknown (`let x = f();` where `f`'s result type is not read, an element of a collection, the result of `?` or `.await`, a field of a type outside the repo), a method of a type outside the repo (also one an `impl` of a trait of the repo adds to a type that is not declared in the repo, as `String`, whose own methods are not known), a call through `Deref`, a field called in parentheses (`(self.f)(x)`), `T::f()` with `T` a type parameter, a call written with a turbofish (`f::<T>()`), `Self::` in a block whose type is not read, a macro-generated function and a glob-imported name are given no edge.

**Go fact sheets (#224).** `FactSheet.language` is the `label` of the symbol's language module (`TypeScript`, `Go`); the worker renders it as a `language:` line after `kind:` and the describer agent receives it in each item. For Go, `doc` is the `//` comment lines directly above the declaration, `returnType` is the result part of the stored signature (`(string, bool)`, `*Store`; null when the function returns nothing), and `callees` come from `calls` edges when the index has them. `contentKey` does not depend on the prompt text, only on `PROMPT_MAJOR`, so the prompt wording change of #224 keeps `p1`.

**Go `init` fact sheets (#372).** For a Go function named `init`, `FactSheet.runsWhen` is the fixed text `Runs once at startup, when its package is loaded.`, written by the host (`GO_INIT_RUNS_WHEN` in `factsheet.ts`) and never taken from the code. The field is absent for every other function. The worker renders it as a `runs:` line before the body, the describer's claim item carries it as `runs_when` (only when set), and `groundingNames` reads its words like the words of a doc comment, so a description of an `init` function may say that it runs at startup without any word being added to the word lists. The field is not part of `contentKey` and no stored row changes (`SCHEMA_VERSION` stays 12).

**contentKey** = `<exactHash>:<PROMPT_MAJOR>`; `PROMPT_MAJOR` starts at `p1`.

**Overlay types (#286; `core/src/overlay/types.ts`, exported from `@gantry/core`).** What a person decided about the map is stored as selectors, not as node lists, and applied when a view is built (section 11, "Overlay at view time"). Zod schemas, all `.strict()`; the types are `z.infer` of them.

```ts
export const NodeIdSchema = z.string().min(1).max(1024);
export const GroupIdSchema = z.string().regex(/^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9-]{0,62}$/);
export const SelectorSchema = z.discriminatedUnion("type", [
  z.object({ type: z.literal("glob"), glob: z.string().min(1).max(300) }).strict(), // repo-relative posix path glob (picomatch, dot: true)
  z.object({ type: z.literal("kind"), kind: z.enum(["workspace","dir","file","function","method","component","hook","class","type","interface","enum","const","route","endpoint","middleware","table"]) }).strict(),
  z.object({ type: z.literal("workspace"), workspace: NodeIdSchema }).strict(), // a workspace id (`ws:...`)
  z.object({ type: z.literal("ids"), ids: z.array(NodeIdSchema).min(1).max(500) }).strict(),
]);
export const FilterSpecSchema = z.object({
  view: z.string().min(1).max(50),
  level: z.enum(["workspaces","dirs","files","symbols"]).optional(),
  focus: NodeIdSchema.optional(),
  hidden: z.array(SelectorSchema).max(50).default([]),  // hidden only in this view, on top of the global hidden list
  groups: z.array(GroupIdSchema).max(50).default([]),   // groups listed in the response; [] = all groups
  filters: z.record(z.string().max(100), z.string().max(500)).default({}),
}).strict();
export const GroupSchema = z.object({
  id: GroupIdSchema,
  name: z.string().min(1).max(80),
  selectors: z.array(SelectorSchema).min(1).max(50),
  mayUse: z.array(GroupIdSchema).max(50).nullable().default(null), // null: no boundary rule
}).strict();
export const SavedViewSchema = z.object({ id: GroupIdSchema, name: z.string().min(1).max(80), spec: FilterSpecSchema }).strict();
export const LabelSchema = z.object({ nodeId: NodeIdSchema, text: z.string().max(80) }).strict(); // text "" removes the label
export type Selector, FilterSpec, Group, SavedView, Label = z.infer<...>;
export interface OverlayState { labels: Label[]; hidden: Selector[]; groups: Group[]; views: SavedView[] }
```

`matchSelector(sel, node: SelectableNode)` and `matchAny(selectors, node)` (`core/src/overlay/select.ts`), with `SelectableNode = { id, kind, path?, workspace? }`: `glob` matches `path` (a symbol's path is its file's path; a directory node also matches when the glob with a trailing `/**` removed matches it), `kind` matches the node kind, `workspace` matches the node's workspace id (a `ws:` node matches itself), `ids` matches `id` exactly. Paths and workspaces come from the store, never from parsing an id. `core/src/overlay/repo.ts` is the only writer of the overlay tables: `readOverlay`, `setLabel`, `setHidden`, `saveGroup`, `deleteGroup` (also removes the group's rule, its id from other groups' `mayUse` and from saved views' `groups`), `saveView`, `deleteView`, `resetOverlay` (clears the five curation tables, keeps dismissals and layouts); each write is one `store.tx`.

**Shared views and groups (`core/src/team/views.ts`, exported from `@gantry/core`).** `mergedGroups(store, cache)` and `mergedViews(store, cache)` return the team's groups (or saved views) from the team cache (`readTeamGroups`, `readTeamViews`; a row that does not parse with `GroupSchema` or `SavedViewSchema` is skipped) plus the local ones the overlay stores (`readOverlay`; the computed default groups are not included), each as `Group & { origin: "team" | "local"; shadowed?: true }` (`SavedView` for views), sorted by `id`, team before local. When a team item and a local item have the same `id` the team item wins: the local one stays in the list with `origin: "local"` and `shadowed: true`, and nothing is written to the overlay. `cache` may be `undefined` (no team data), which gives the local items alone. `defaultViewId(cache)` is `settings.default_view` of the cache when a cached view has that id, else `undefined`. These three are synchronous readers with no network, so a hook may import them (the end-of-turn note uses `mergedGroups` and `readTeamRepo`, the reader of `meta.repo`, for team boundaries, section 14). `publishView(client, repo, view, refresh)`, `publishGroup(client, repo, group, refresh)`, `unpublishView(client, repo, id, refresh)` and `unpublishGroup(client, repo, id, refresh)` call the team client's `PUT` or `DELETE` of `/v1/repos/:repo/views/:id` or `/groups/:id` (section 20; `repo` is `owner/name`, URL-encoded), then call `refresh` (the command passes `pullTeamCache`), so the cache shows the change at once. A refusal is thrown as the client's `GantryError` carrying the server's `message`; no hook imports these four (section 14). The unpublish functions answer `{ deleted, refresh }`, where `deleted` is false when the team had no such id.

**Default groups (#290; `core/src/overlay/defaults.ts`, `repo.ts`).** `DEFAULT_GROUP_GLOBS = ["apps/*/src/features/*", "apps/*/src/modules/*", "packages/*"]` (the default of the former slices config key; that key is gone and an old config's `views` key is dropped like any unknown key). `defaultGroups(files: FileRecord[]): Group[]` returns one group per directory that matches a glob and holds at least one of `files`: `id` = `default:<dir>`, `name` = `<dir>`, `selectors: [{ type: "glob", glob: "<dir>/**" }]`, `mayUse: null`, sorted by id. Default groups are computed on every read and never written to `overlay.db`; the `default:` prefix is reserved because `GroupIdSchema` rejects `:`. `effectiveGroups(store): Group[]` is the user groups (by id) followed by the default groups for `store.effectiveFiles()`. A file belongs to the first group that matches it: user groups first, then the deepest default directory.

**Impact tiers (#293; `core/src/review/consumers.ts`, `impact.ts`).** A change's consumers come in three tiers: `direct` (hop 1), `potential` (hops 2 and 3) and `reachable` (hop 4 and later). `Consumer` gains two optional fields, set on `reachable` entries only so that `direct` and `potential` entries serialise as before: `pathConfidence?: "high" | "low"` and `fanIn?: number`. `ChangeConsumers` gains `reachable: Consumer[]` and `reachableTotal: number`. `Impact` gains `reachable: { total: number; byHop: { hop: number; count: number }[] }`. Section 11 defines the walk and the ranking. **Hub entries (#294).** `ChangeConsumers` gains `hubs: HubEntry[]` and `Impact` gains `hubs: { hub: string; consumersTotal: number }[]`. `HubEntry = { hub (node id), name, kind, path, workspace (name or null), hop (of the consumers it stands for: the hub's own hop + 1), consumersTotal (uncapped), byWorkspace: { workspace: string | null; count: number }[] (sorted by workspace, none last), members: Consumer[] (those consumers in `compareReachable` order, at most 200) }`. Section 11 defines when a node is a hub and which consumers collapse.

**Team layer types (explorer; `explorer/server/types.ts`, mirrored in `explorer/web/src/api.ts`).** `GET /api/team/layer` (section 11) answers `TeamLayerResponse = { available: boolean, age_s: number | null, change_sets: TeamChangeSet[], people: string[] }` with `TeamChangeSet = { id, source: "pr" | "branch", state: "proposed" | "in_progress" | "in_review" | "merged", owner (a GitHub login), title?, pr?, nodes: string[], radius: { id, hop, tier: "direct" | "potential" | "reachable", via_hub }[], nodes_total, radius_total, map_nodes: string[], direct_uses: { id: string, changed: string[] }[] }`. `nodes` holds at most 200 ids (sorted) and `radius` at most 500 entries, lowest hop first; `nodes_total` and `radius_total` count them before the cut. `map_nodes` is the workspace id of each changed node (a symbol's file's workspace, or the file's), deduplicated and sorted, resolved against the local index; a node id the index does not know, or one outside every workspace, is counted in `nodes_total` and left out of `map_nodes`. `direct_uses` says which changed nodes each directly affected entry uses, by the local graph (`ctx.store`): for each hop-1 radius entry that is a symbol or file, `changed` holds the ids of `nodes` that the entry, or the file it is in, has an edge to; an entry with no such edge in the local index (the index differs from the uploader's) is left out. It is sorted by `id`, `changed` is sorted, and it is computed from the uncut `nodes` and `radius`. `owner` and `title` come from the server and are untrusted text.

## 8. Description schema and checks

JSON Schema (used for `--json-schema`, MCP submit validation and tests): `behavior` string, ≤ 25 words and ≤ 200 chars; `searchTerms` 1–6 strings, each ≤ 30 chars; `nameFits` boolean; `suggestedName` required iff `nameFits` is false, must match `^[A-Za-z_$][\w$]*$`.
Hard caps come first and are O(1), before any regex or normalisation: raw `behavior` ≤ 400 chars, each search term ≤ 60 chars, `suggestedName` ≤ 80 chars, ≤ 6 terms; over a cap fails with reason `length` and is never repaired. So no scanner ever sees more than 400 characters of `behavior`, and every regex is linear (no nested quantifiers).
The validator accepts the **shape of a description** instead of banning phrases (a phrase list cannot be both tight and precise; the independent review of #22 failed the first list on injection bypasses and 18–23% false rejects). Failure reasons: `schema`, `length`, `shape`, `identifier`, `injection`, `secret`. Checks run per function, in order:

1. **Hard caps** (above), then **schema** (types, `suggestedName` rule).
2. **Secret filter** (`secret`) on `behavior`, each term, the fields joined, and `suggestedName`: vendor key shapes (AWS, GitHub, GitLab, Slack, Stripe, OpenAI/Anthropic, Google, npm, Hugging Face, SendGrid, Twilio, Mailgun, Shopify, Vercel, DigitalOcean, PyPI, Linear, Notion, Telegram, Discord, Sentry, Square, Azure, `AGE-SECRET-KEY-1…`), hex runs ≥ 32, private keys, JWTs, `scheme://user:password@host` and `scheme://:password@host` with any scheme, `Authorization: Basic …`, `Bearer` tokens, `Set-Cookie`/`session=` values ≥ 16 chars, string literals assigned to a **credential name** (below), unquoted `NAME=value` and `name: value`, high-entropy tokens and base64-wrapped keys. A second pass removes hyphens between alphanumerics and joins adjacent tokens, so keys split by hyphens, spaces or across fields are found. Details name rules, never matched text.
   Credential name = an identifier or quoted key whose word parts (camelCase, snake_case, kebab-case, dots) include `password`, `passwd`, `pwd`, `pass`, `secret`, `token`, `credential(s)`, `apikey`, `passphrase`, or `key` **only** next to `api`, `secret`, `access`, `private`, `signing`, `sign`, `auth`, `encryption`, `master` or `license` (so `key`, `sortKey`, `cacheKey`, `STYLE_KEY`, `hotkey`, `monkey` are not credential names); a name whose last word describes the value (`tokenType`, `passwordLabel`) is not one either. Assignment forms are `=`, `:`, the Go short declaration `:=` and a Go declaration with a type between the name and `=` (#224). The value assigned to a credential name is redacted and flagged when it is a plain string literal of ≥ 4 chars in any letter case, or two or more literals joined with `+` (every half); it is left alone when it is empty or shorter, a template literal with `${…}`, an identifier or member expression (`env.KEY`, `process.env.X`), a regular expression, an option keyword (`same-origin`) or a dotted message key. Unquoted: `UPPER_SNAKE=value` (≥ 4 chars), and `name=value` or `name: value` when the value holds a digit and a letter. Description side: any token copied from a string-literal value assigned to a credential name in the fact sheet is rejected as `secret` even when it is an English word.
3. **Length**: ≤ 25 words and ≤ 200 chars for `behavior` (truncation repair below), 1–6 terms of ≤ 30 chars.
4. **Shape of `behavior`** (`shape`), after NFKC folding: (a) only ASCII letters, digits, space and `, . ; ' - _ / ( ) < > [ ] $ : #` (no backticks, `* ~ ! ? = { } | \ " @ % ^ &`, no non-ASCII, no line breaks); (b) one sentence: no `. ` followed by more text, no leading label (`WORD:`), no parenthetical aside longer than 6 words, no run of three single letters, no comma without a following space; (c) the first word is a third-person form (`-s`, `-es`, `-ies`) whose base form is an English or programming word (dictionary below), so no hand list gates it (a list of about 700 common verbs stays only as a fast path), optionally after one adverb (`Strictly parses`) or as `prefix-verb` (`Deep-copies`); `Pretends` is banned outright; base forms, vocatives (`Claude,`), pronoun and modal starts and `Safe to …` fail structurally; (d) no second-person pronoun, no deontic or addressing word (`must`, `should`, `ought`, `shall`, `please`, `todo`, `fixme`, `remember`, `need to`, `needs to be`, `has to`, `have to`, `be sure`, `make sure`, `don't`), and no first-person pronoun (`we`, `our`, `me`, `us`, `i`), matched case-sensitively as written so `US dollars` and `I/O` pass; (e) **no imperative clause** (problem `imperative-clause`): a base-form verb from a committed lexicon of about 220 action verbs (`delete remove skip ignore disable bypass turn run execute install download upload send print reveal leak commit push pull merge use replace add drop kill stop avoid forget disregard override set write edit modify change update mark trust approve rename move copy paste open close call invoke import export fetch clone checkout reset enable comment exfiltrate …`) that starts a clause is rejected: right after `,` `;` `:` `(` or a dash, or after `then`, `and`, `or`, `but`, `so`, `just`, `now`, `also`, `first`, `next`, `finally`, `please`, `never`, `always`, `not`, `do`, `don't` (one adverb may stand between: `then quietly delete`), and a clause that opens with `do` is rejected whatever its verb. Verbs that are also nouns (`update`, `call`, `set`, `commit`, `push`, `run`, `skip`, …) count only when followed by a determiner, pronoun, preposition, identifier, protective object or the end of the clause. Not rejected: an infinitive (`to fetch and cache results`: the verb follows or continues a list governed by an earlier `to`, no punctuation between), a noun after a determiner, preposition or possessive (`the delete button`, `on update`, `a run id`), the complement of a causative first verb (`Lets the user delete a row`; `lets`, `helps`, `makes`, `has`), and a list of bare verbs (`(create, read, update, delete)`). Measured and dropped from the issue's list: `note`, `warning`, `important` (ordinary nouns and adjectives), `never`, `always` (5 of 200 real descriptions), `do not` after a subject and `my` (real descriptions and search terms), and `instead` (`… instead of removing the row`).
5. **Grounding** (`identifier`), dictionary based. Every token of `behavior`, of each search term and of each split word of `suggestedName` must be (a) an **English or programming word** (the hand lists include acronyms, platform names and ordinary words the 1934 list lacks, such as `E2E`, `UTM`, `PostHog`, `allowlist`, `roadmap`, `rowid`, `classify`, and the SQLite `json_*` functions): in the shipped word list, on the hand list of ordinary terms, or a regular derived form of one (plural `-s/-es/-ies`, `-ed`, `-ing` with doubling and e-drop, `-er/-est`, `-ly`, `-able/-ible`, `-ness`, `-ment`, `-tion`, `-ity`, …; prefixes `un- re- non- pre- de- sub- multi- auto-` and a few more, two steps deep), (b) a number of fewer than 7 digits, a clock time or a size such as `30s`, `4x` (a decimal such as `1.5` too, except right after `to`, `from`, `at`, `on`, `host`, `ip`, …; three or more small numbers in a row are rejected), (c) **grounded in the fact sheet**: the whole token is an identifier, path, file name, signature name, callee, return-type name or doc identifier, or it is a sub-word of one (camelCase, PascalCase, snake_case, kebab-case and path segments, file names and extensions split into lower-case parts; `handleStripeWebhook` grounds `stripe` and `webhook`, `later-work-controller.ts` grounds `later-work`), or (d) on the stop list. Anything else is rejected, which covers foreign-language text, run-on bait names (`leftpadsecure`), spelled-out hosts (`rs`, `sh`, `com` are not words), copied lowercase passwords and opaque tokens. The camelCase and snake_case forms must be whole names of the fact sheet. **Compounds** joined by a hyphen or one slash are accepted only when on the ordinary-English allow-list (about 200: `read-only`, `built-in`, `sign-in`, `AES-256-GCM`, `robots.txt`, …), matching a small set of productive patterns (`non-`/`multi-`/`cross-`/`semi-`/`anti-`/`sub-` plus a word, a word plus `-based`/`-only`/`-safe`/`-aware`/`-readable`/…, a noun plus a participle such as `size-limited`, a compound with a function word such as `waiting-for-review`), grounded as a whole, or when **every** part is grounded in the fact sheet; two dictionary words are not enough (`evil-pad`, `auth/bypass`, `pastebin-mirror`, `leftpad-secure` fail). The ordinary-English allow-list also holds `schema.org` and the common security response header names (`Strict-Transport-Security`, `Content-Security-Policy`, ...). Route literals (`/rpc/`) and `re-` compounds (`re-validates`) are not accepted: a path or a prefix can hide a verb (`/nuke/ the suite`, `Re-elevates privileges`). A dotted or host-like token is grounded only by a whole identifier, path, callee or relative-import file name, never by a string literal, a default parameter value, a comment or a doc dotted chain; the single literal values that ground are `UPPER_SNAKE` constants such as `NOT_FOUND`. Every input the grounding scan reads is capped before any regex: signature ≤ 400 chars, doc ≤ 2,000, body ≤ 8,000. Failure details echo the first 12 characters of an offending token followed by `…`.
   **Word list**: `packages/core/src/describe/wordlist.data.ts`, generated by `scripts/build-wordlist.ts` from `/usr/share/dict/words` (Webster's Second International, 234,936 entries, 1934; its README says "The 1934 copyright has lapsed", so the list is public domain): lower-cased alphabetic words of 4–20 letters, 232,843 words, stored as a Bloom filter (15 bits per word, 10 hashes, 0.07% measured false positives, 582 KB of base64 in a TS module bundled by tsup, no file I/O, decoded lazily on first use). Two- and three-letter words and about 1,700 programming and web terms the 1934 list lacks (`async`, `callback`, `middleware`, `webhook`, `oauth`, `regex`, `json`, `email`, `database`, `timeout`, …) are hand lists in `wordlist.ts`. Adding a word means adding it to that list, with a test row that needs it.
   Search terms: 1–4 words each (more is a benign case: the term is stripped and the repair `search-terms-stripped` reported), all words grounded as above, no addressing words, no speech act or agent subject (check 6), no secret value, none starting with `prefer`, `avoid`, `please` or a shell or package-manager command (`npm`, `pnpm`, `yarn`, `npx`, `pip`, `curl`, `wget`, `sudo`, `chmod`; `curl snippet` style two-word names pass), no single-word `disregard`/`forget`.
